journal carrying 24 `decision` events -- **the column that was empty in all
four of Fran's recording sessions**, filled because the checkpoint now loads at
the current observation width.

Three combats reached rounds **7, 4 and 1**: mean 4.0, 67% lasting past round
3, against a simulator mean of 2.0 and 9.2%. n=3 and directional, but it is an
independent replication of Fran's own 10-combat sample (3.9, 50%) with a
different party in a different session, and it lands on his number rather than
the simulator's. See `tools/stress_ratchet.py`.

### A reference that could NOT be banked, and why that is the right answer

The tutorial's victory-results screen was unrecognised (best `raid_results` at
0.175, runner-up `raid_results_defeat` at 0.273 -- inside THRESHOLD but a
near-tie the MARGIN test correctly refuses), which stalled the walk back to
town for 16 s. Banking it as `raid_results@old_road` fixed the walk and then
failed `test_every_screen_is_far_from_every_other_one`, which wants every
screen `2 * MARGIN` from every other: it sat 0.273 from `raid_results_defeat`.

**The variant was removed rather than the bar lowered.** The frame was a dark
mid-fade (mean brightness 10), which compresses the signature and pulls every
screen together, so it was a bad reference -- and the bar it failed is the one
that stops a DEFEAT being read as a VICTORY, which would send the loop down the
wrong chain. `test_every_screen_is_far_from_every_other_one`'s own docstring
already says the answer to a crowded region is a second discriminating region,
never a smaller MARGIN. **What is needed is a settled frame of that screen**,
captured a few seconds after the raid ends.

## The demonstration corpus: `bridge/journal.py` (2026-09-03)

Fran: *"Improve the advise model so it gathers much more data and ill just run
it and play through several expeditions, make sure it logs everything, dungeon,
hamlet, team management, money management etc."*

```bash
bat\combat_advise.bat                    # play; it watches and records
python tools/journal_report.py           # what the corpus caught, mid-session
python tools/journal_report.py --differs # every disagreement, to annotate
```

`--log FILE` now also writes `FILE.jsonl`: **one JSON object per event**, beside
the readable log rather than instead of it. `--journal none` opts out. The prose
log is for a person; every analysis built on it so far has had to regex it back
into structure and **two of those regexes were wrong in one session** — a prose
log is not a dataset.

**Three things were missing and all three are load-bearing.**

- **THE HAMLET WAS NEVER RECORDED AT ALL.** `_watch` keys on
  `persist.raid.json` and `continue`s whenever the save says the party is not
  in a raid, so every town visit ever made went past it. The town poll now runs
  **first, outside every raid-signature early return** — putting it after them
  is the same as not writing it, because that file does not move while the
  party is in the Hamlet. Five stat calls on `TOWN_FILES`.
- **The town is DIFFED, not read.** Two `TownState` snapshots plus the upgrade
  ledger, subtracted, is a complete account of a town visit with no screen
  reading and no actuator: wallet deltas, hires, departures, treatment,
  resolve, trinkets, upgrade trees, the quest map. Keyed on hero **guid**, never
  roster position — the roster reorders freely and a positional diff reports one
  hire as four changes. Every branch records the **before and the after**, not
  the delta: "gold −1500" cannot answer "could he have afforded the other
  thing", which is the question a spend is about.
- **RECORDING WHAT WAS PLAYED WAS FUSED TO GRADING IT.** `_resolve` returns
  immediately when `_pending` is None, and `_arm` leaves it None when the
  advisor has nothing to say — which is every turn while no checkpoint loads.
  So the combat corpus would have been **empty in exactly the state that makes
  it most valuable**. `_record_play` is the split: `play` events need no policy,
  `decision` events are the agreement half. Measured on the replay corpus:
  **58 demonstrations recorded model-less, up from 0.**

**A `play` carries the BOARD, because advise mode captures no saves.** HP,
stress, affliction and virtue per hero; enemy id, HP, slot and corpse flag;
torch. Whatever is not in that event is gone — `--replay` reads
`archive/traces/`, but a live watch writes nothing else.

**`hero_left` does not say why.** The town save cannot tell a death from a
dismissal, so the event records the departure and refuses to guess; pairing it
with the raid's own outcome is a reader's job.

## A checkpoint at the wrong width now REFUSES instead of raising per turn

`Advisor.__init__` compares the checkpoint's observation width against the
env's and drops the model with one clear line if they differ, setting
`model_refused`. SB3 does not notice until `predict` — which is the first combat
turn, mid-raid, once per turn, forever.

That is not a hypothetical: the width is a property of the **loaded data** as
well as of the code (the enemy-type one-hot), so a pure loader fix reached it on
2026-09-02 with no change in `env/` at all. Refusing costs the combat advice and
keeps everything else — route, torch, curio and camp decide without a policy,
and the journal still records every move played. That is the difference between
a usable recording session and an exception per turn.

`_Pending` is a `NamedTuple` now rather than a bare tuple, with `board`
defaulted: the shape is built in `_arm`, read in `_resolve`, and built a third
time by hand in `tests/bridge/test_run.py`, where adding a sixth element broke an
unpack a long way from the change.

## THE RULERS ON THE NEW CHECKPOINT, AND A FIGHT-LENGTH GAP (2026-09-03)

The four expert rulers had never run against a 1652-wide model. All of them
now have. Read as corroboration, never as targets.

**`tools/agreement.py` self-check PASSES** — every delta exactly zero, so any
delta it reports is the checkpoints and not the harness. Its algorithmic layers
come back **identical** to the recorded values (route 80.0%, torch 66.7%, curio
85.7%), which is the right answer: those layers do not consult a checkpoint, so
identical is the consistency check passing. Combat reads 53.5% (n=101) against
39.6 / 47.5 / 35.6% for earlier checkpoints — **and that number is still
disqualified**, because the corpus was recorded while Fran was testing the
bridge rather than playing to win. Do not read it as "better".

### `team_probe`: the control criticism has flipped sign

| | old (1636) | new |
|---|---|---|
| FOCUS | +7.3pp | **+9.6pp** |
| FINISH | −20.0pp | **−26.2pp** |
| CONTROL | **−4.8pp** | **+2.0pp** |

Control being taken below its share of the bar was "the half of Fran's review
that survives". It is now taken 2.0pp ABOVE its share. Not a clean A/B — the
engine and the training distribution both moved — but it is the same tool on
the same scenario config.

### `check_class_play`: the policy heals MORE than before and still less than Fran

| Crusader heal share | |
|---|---|
| 2026-08-27 | 51.9% |
| shipped (1636), re-measured 08-30 | 46.0% |
| **new checkpoint** | **52.6%** |
| **Fran's real play** | **73.3%** |

**"The policy over-heals" remains unsupported by real play**, in the same
direction as before and now with both sides measured on comparable tools.

What the per-class table does show is **one skill per class**: `shieldbreaker`
plays `pierce` on 96.3% of its turns, `houndmaster` `hounds_rush` 83.0%,
`abomination` `manacles` 65.6%, `arbalest` two skills and nothing else. And
`crusader/inspiring_cry` — an A-tier stress heal — is played on **1.4%** of
Crusader turns while `battle_heal` takes 51.2%.

### `style_probe`: within-category choice is now BELOW chance, not at it

LOW **−4.2pp**, MID **−16.9pp**, HIGH **−2.2pp** against its own random
baseline. There is no gradient, so the 2026-08-30 retraction of "it degrades as
sustain rises" stands. What does not survive is its replacement — "about as
good as chance at every sustain level" — since MID is well below.

### THE FINDING: simulator fights are HALF as long as real ones

| | real play | simulator |
|---|---|---|
| combats | 10 | 207 |
| mean rounds | **3.9** | **2.0** |
| median | 4 | 2 |
| lasting >3 rounds | **50.0%** | **9.2%** |

`check_playbook` independently reports mean 2.0 / >3 rounds 8.6% over 325
combats, so the simulator side replicates.

**This is the structural explanation the "one plan" criticism has been missing.**
PLAYBOOK 3.5's whole point is that buffs need long fights to pay off. In a
two-round fight a buff, a control skill or a stress heal cannot repay its turn
— so a policy that skips them is being CORRECT about the world it was trained
in, and the expert tier list is right about a *different* world. The two rulers
have been disagreeing for weeks and this would reconcile them.

It also bears on the ratchet: shorter fights mean less stress per combat, which
is a different stress economy from the real game's.

**DIRECTIONAL, and doubly so.** Ten real combats. And Fran's party was a
low-level tier-1 roster whose damage is weak, which lengthens fights by itself
— the simulator sample is not tier-matched. **A bigger recording at a matched
tier is the next step, not an engine edit.** `python tools/stress_ratchet.py`
prints both mechanics.

## `--affliction-penalty` IS HARMFUL, and the mechanism is the ratchet (2026-09-03)

Four 3M runs at the shipped recipe — control and `--affliction-penalty 20`,
seeds 0 and 1 — each scored `--all-comps --episodes 4` over 176 comps. The
prior was null, because three of the last four single-knob reward experiments
were. **It is not null. It is actively worse, and it replicates.**

| | s0 | s1 | mean of seeds | between-seed spread |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| SUCCESS | −7.50 | −6.60 | **−7.05** | 0.90 |
| QUALITY | −1.47 | −2.67 | **−2.07** | 1.20 |

The effect on SUCCESS is **eight times** the spread between seeds, which is the
bar `--stress-weight` and `--prevention-reward` both failed. Per band it is
worst where the runs are longest: long-band SUCCESS **−11.8 / −10.4**.

**Six of six band-seed comparisons agree on SUCCESS, on QUALITY, and on HP.
Deaths go the other way, six of six: +0.15 on the long band.**

### The damning column is stress, which did not move

| | s0 | s1 |
|---|---|---|
| stress, long band | −0.6 | +4.8 |

Three of six negative — no consistent movement at all. An affliction is what
happens at 100 stress, so a term that charges for afflictions should show up
as *less stress* if the policy learned to avoid them. It did not learn to avoid
them. It just got worse.

### Why: it charges for something the policy cannot prevent

20 is 40% of `REWARD_HERO_DEATH`, levied once per afflicted hero. If
afflictions are largely unavoidable, that is not a signal — it is **variance**
added to the return with no controllable component, and PPO's advantage
estimates get noisier for nothing.

And the same day's ratchet measurement says they largely are unavoidable:
stress recovery is weak even in the simulator (falls **−6.9** against rises
**+12.6**, and only 48% of heroes above 50 ever come back — 14% in real play).
A hero climbing toward 100 mostly keeps climbing. **Charging for the arrival
does not buy a way to avoid it.**

The rise in deaths is consistent with the policy trying anyway: ending runs
faster is the one lever it has against accumulating stress, and haste costs
heroes.

### The theory point, which is the transferable part

`--prevention-reward` and the `W_*` terms sit **inside Φ**, so by
Ng/Harada/Russell they cannot change the optimal policy and can only shift
credit timing — which is exactly why they came back null, twice. This term is
**non-potential**, so it genuinely can move the optimum, and it did. **Moving
the optimum is not the same as improving it.** A reward term that prices a real
thing correctly still hurts if the agent has no policy that avoids it.

Default stays **0.0**. The flag stays, with this result attached, so nobody
re-runs it hoping.

**What would change the answer**: make afflictions preventable first. That is
the stress-recovery question the ratchet opened, not a reward-tuning question —
and it is a fidelity investigation, not a knob.

## THE POST-RETRAIN SESSION: A MISSING CONTROL, TWO SHUT GATES, AND A RATCHET (2026-09-03)

The 12M retrain landed (2h27m, LR out to 1.7e-07, `clip_fraction` 0) and
`bridge/advisor.py` advises again. Everything below came out of trying to read
its eval honestly.

### There is no control, and there cannot be one

The new checkpoint reads 96.4 / 94.7 / **81.7** SUCCESS against the archived
97.6 / 96.6 / **90.1**. That looks like an 8.4pp regression on the long band
and it is **not a measurable claim**. Three things moved at once: the
2026-09-02 fidelity batch (harder on purpose), the comp pool (169 → 176), and
the observation width — and the last is why the obvious check is impossible.
**A 1636 checkpoint does not load on a 1652 engine.** The previous plan's step
"run both on today's engine" cannot be executed.

`CLAUDE.md`'s own rule already covered this — *"either retrain the arm, or run
the old checkpoint in a worktree at the pre-change commit"* — and the worktree
route only reproduces the archived number, which is not a control for the new
world either. So the honest record is **"new baseline"**, and the old table now
sits under a heading that dates it.

### The eval scores an EASIER world than training, by 1.4 to 5.7pp

Chasing the drop found something better. The underleveled-hero penalty — the
2026-09-02 headline fix — **does not fire during evaluation at all**, behind two
independent gates:

1. `eval_expedition` builds its env with no `resolve_sampling`, so
   `TIER_TO_HERO_LEVEL` pins every hero at the top of its bracket and the
   shortfall against `TIER_TO_QUEST_DIFFICULTY` is zero in every bracket.
2. The explicit-scenario path leaves `quest_difficulty_level` `None`, and
   `CombatEngine.reset` reads `None` as zero shortfall. The SAMPLED path, which
   is what training uses, fills it from `TIER_TO_QUEST_DIFFICULTY`.

**Opening one alone measures nothing**, and that is how the second gate was
found: an `--all-comps` run passing only `quest_difficulty_level` came back
**byte-identical** to the run without it — 96.4/94.7/81.7 on both. A no-op that
produces plausible output is exactly the failure this repo keeps finding, and
the only thing that caught it was comparing rather than assuming.

`--underleveled` opens both, default off so archived numbers stay reproducible.
Paired on identical scenarios:

| band | as shipped | in the TRAINING world | delta |
|---|---|---|---|
| short | 96.4 / 90.0 | 95.0 / 87.7 | −1.4 / −2.3 |
| medium | 94.7 / 85.9 | 91.8 / 83.3 | **−2.9** / −2.6 |
| long | 81.7 / 75.1 | **76.0 / 71.8** | **−5.7** / −3.3 |

Long-band stress p90 **51.5 → 69.0**; episodes ending a hero over 100, 4.0% →
4.7%. First measurement of that fix, and among the largest effects recorded
here. It does **not** explain the drop against 90.1, which was also taken with
the penalty off.

### Stress is a RATCHET, and both obvious readings of it were wrong

Fran, watching a raid fall apart: *"when a hero starts panicking usually its
like a house of cards in a sense"*. `tools/stress_ratchet.py` grades that
against his own recordings and against the simulator, and it corrected me
twice.

**It does not accelerate.** Rises from under 50 average **+13.1** a turn; from
50 or over, **+10.6** — slightly slower.

**It is not irreversible either**, which I had asserted. Stress comes down 34
times in his corpus and 1 of the 7 heroes who crossed 50 got back under. I had
looked only at max-versus-end for the heroes that crossed, which is a different
question and a worse one.

What is lopsided is the **size**: falls average **−4.4** against rises of
**+12.4**. Reversible in principle, priced so it never keeps up.

And the simulator disagrees about exactly that half:

| | real play (n=8 heroes) | simulator (n=368) |
|---|---|---|
| P(recover) above 50 | **14%** | **48%** |
| mean fall | **−4.4** | **−6.9** |
| mean rise under 50 | +13.1 | +12.7 |
| mean rise at/over 50 | +10.6 | +12.6 |

**The rises agree and the recoveries do not.** That is a directional finding,
not a measured effect: the real arm is 8 heroes, and the two arms sample at
different rates (per recorded decision against per env step), which cuts in the
direction of finding more dips in the simulator. It says where to look. **A
bigger, rate-matched recording is the next step, not an engine edit** — the
DEBUFF RES precedent is that a guessed fix does not ship.

Why it matters beyond a description: stress is half of `_run_quality`, and
`--stress-weight` came back as a clean HP-for-stress trade at par. If recovery
is genuinely three times cheaper in the simulator than in the game, that trade
is an artefact and the knob was measuring the wrong world.

### The survival floor, scaled by what is actually at risk

Fran's Shambler raid is a counterexample to the floor shipped the same day on
his own instruction: a level-1 party, the Highwayman died, he took
`ancestors_scroll` (`limit=1`) and judged it *"very much worth it"*. The floor
refuses that unconditionally, and `advise_retreat` cannot see the trinket at
all — trinkets are deliberately unpriced.

It is the same defect `tools/exchange_rates.py` found one layer down: **a
constant where the game varies by three orders of magnitude.** A level-1
Highwayman is 0 gold sunk; a Week-22 veteran is 23,750.

`scale_floor_by_investment` (default **False**) applies the floor only when a
hero is worth more than the quest being risked for them. **The threshold is
derived, not invented** — `mean_sunk > forfeited`, both already in gold from
shipped files — so the flag adds no number to tune, and on Fran's scene it
reproduces his call while still firing for a kitted party.

## WHY IT DIED: THE STAY/LEAVE LAYER NEVER SAID NO (2026-09-03)

Fran: *"using the model in game I had several deaths which I didn't really
understand why it went for that kind of play"*, and *"I don't really know what
should be the optimal scoring parameters for the model as well as weights"*.

The first half turned out to have a precise answer, and it was **not** the
combat policy. `bat/play.log` records the model's own survival forecast beside
every decision, and at 13:23:03 on 2026-09-02 it reads:

    [13:23:03] [R1] Shieldbreaker (rank 3, 12/22 hp, 152 stress)
      10 combats left in crypts (tier 1): P(finish intact) 0% +-0, expected deaths 3.8
      leaving costs ~6,278 gold, staying ~-375 - worth pushing on

**It predicted the whole party would die and priced staying as a net GAIN of
375 gold.** Over 514 logged decisions the verdict is "worth pushing on" at every
survival probability from 0% to 100%, including **130 of 135 decisions at
P(finish intact) <= 25%**. RETREAT was advised 5 times in 514 (1.0%), all five
at 0%. A rule that insensitive to its own forecast is not a rule.

The combat play in those scenes is unremarkable -- it attacks the Shambler, the
Crusader is offered Battle Heal. The macro layer was telling it the run was
profitable, for three reasons.

### 1. A death was priced at ~1/30 of its value

`bridge/advisor.py` called `advise_retreat(state.heroes, result, self._reward)`
with **no `profile` and no `store`**, so `party_investment` took the fallback
branch that its own docstring calls *"wrong by a factor of thirty on a real
campaign: measured over Fran's Week 22 roster, 750 gold against 23,750"*. The
accurate path (`data/hero_value.party_value`, reading `persist.upgrades.json`)
had been written, tested and never wired to the only caller that runs live.

At the fallback price 3.8 expected deaths is ~2,850 gold. At the real one it is
~90,250, and the comparison is not close.

**The fallback is now an actual floor**: with a profile the per-hero figure is
`max(profile reading, equipped reading)`. The profile path is normally the far
larger, so coming back *under* the equipment floor means the parse lost
something rather than that the hero is cheap -- and `persist.upgrades.json` is
exactly the file that invites this, since `requirement_code` and `is_purchased`
decode to nothing on all 228 entries of a real save.

The profile is taken from the raid save's own directory, **and only when that
directory holds `persist.upgrades.json`**. A replayed capture in
`archive/traces/` sits beside no such file; pointing `party_value` at one would
price the party off whatever it happened to find.

### 2. The stress still ahead was priced at exactly ZERO -- a units bug

`mean_final_stress` is a **per-hero mean** (`eval_expedition.py`, a mean over
the living heroes). `carried` was a **party SUM**. So

    extra_stress = max(0.0, mean_final_stress - carried)

clamped to 0 for any party carrying real stress -- the median logged decision
sits at 36 stress a head, so it was 0 essentially always. The same function
scaled the *retreat* side by `len(living)` and the *continue* side not at all;
the inconsistency was visible inside eleven lines.

**Every test fixture in `tests/bridge/test_retreat.py` defaults to `stress=0.0`** --
where a sum and a mean coincide -- which is why eighteen tests never saw it.
`test_losing_a_rank_zero_party_is_not_worth_retreating_over` was worse than
blind: it passed *because* the stress term was dead, and its scenario loaded
100 stress a head that now correctly dominates the death term it claims to
test. Its docstring now says so, and it holds stress neutral.

Both sides are party totals now.

### 3. The reward is credited on `completion_rate`, not `success_rate`

A run that reaches the end having lost heroes still gets paid, which is
faithful -- the game does pay you. Combined with (1) and (2) it is what makes
"0% intact -> push on" reachable at all, and it is why the repair is a floor on
top of the arithmetic rather than a change to it.

**The survival floor** (`FLOOR_DEATH_CHANCE = 0.5`) refuses to continue when
the rollout says the run is more likely than not to cost somebody, whatever the
gold says. Fran's call, from his rule that an afflicted hero is "almost the same
as a death". It reads `zero_death_rate` -- P(nobody dies), the exact quantity --
and falls back to `mean_deaths >= 0.5` only for a result object that predates
the field; the two are separate constants because an expected COUNT is a
different thing from a probability, and `mean_deaths 1.33` with
`zero_death_rate 0.67` is a real case where they disagree.

`RetreatAdvice.decided_by` records which rule fired, and the floor is named
**only when it changed the answer** -- crediting the override with a call the
gold already made would misattribute it. The log line prints the reason when the
floor decides, because two gold figures that did not decide anything are how a
reader ends up debugging the wrong half.

### The ruler: `tools/retreat_audit.py`

The verdict-by-survival table, the crisis counts, and `--explain` to re-price
one logged decision. A ruler like `skill_tiers`/`team_probe`/`play_log_probe`:
nothing under `engine/`, `env/`, `data/`, `bridge/` or `train.py` may import it,
and a test enforces that.

**Two parsing traps, each of which cost a wrong answer on the first pass**, and
both are in the fixture rather than a tidied sample:

- **`+-0` is what both ends of the distribution print.** A pattern needing two
  digits after the separator silently drops all 110 fully-confident and all 54
  hopeless decisions -- i.e. every row the audit is about. The first ad-hoc pass
  reported a histogram that stopped at 83% and never mentioned 0% or 100%.
- **`[R1] after 1 enemy turn -> Shieldbreaker` is a hero header.** Missing the
  prefix under-counted Death's Door turns **7 against 14** and reported the
  corpus's worst stress as **152 when it is 180** -- and every miss was a hero
  in trouble, because that prefix appears exactly when the enemies got to act
  between your turns. `tools/play_log_probe.py` had already learned this on the
  same log.

### What this does NOT fix

The live bridge still cannot run a model (observation 1652, checkpoints 1636),
so the end-to-end replay gate is blocked on the retrain. The repair is verified
by unit tests and by re-pricing the logged scenes, not by a live run.

## THE WEIGHTS QUESTION: THE GAME'S RATIO IS NOT A CONSTANT (2026-09-03)

`tools/exchange_rates.py` answers *"what should the scoring parameters be"* by
refusing to guess. `--stress-weight` already lowered stress and traded HP for it
at par, buying no score; turning one knob moves the policy ALONG a tradeoff
curve rather than up it, which is why three single-knob sweeps have come back
null. So put both scales side by side instead. How many points of stress is one
hero's life worth?

| | ratio |
|---|---|
| **training reward** | **3,333 : 1 to 5,000 : 1** (one constant, whoever the hero is) |
| game, fresh recruit | **0 : 1** (free from the coach, PLAYBOOK 0.1) |
| game, rank-2 crusader | 394 : 1 |
| game, rank-4 crusader | 1,474 : 1 |
| game, measured Week 22 hero | 1,069 : 1 |

(Un-upgraded Abbey at 22.2 gold a point; a fully upgraded one at 10.0 roughly
doubles every game row, to a maximum of 3,275 : 1.)

**The game varies this by three orders of magnitude and the reward has one
number for all of it -- sitting at or above the top of the range.** So the
policy is trained to treat every hero as a max-investment veteran, including the
resolve-0 Apprentice parties `TIER_TO_RESOLVE_WEIGHTS` draws 20% of the time.
Over-protecting a cheap hero is not a free error: it buys caution with turns and
turns cost stress, which is precisely Fran's *"plays too slow and gets
stress-defeated"* worry, and it is consistent with `--stress-weight` reading as
a straight HP-for-stress trade.

Separately, a completed expedition is worth **0.20 of a hero** in the reward and
about **0.10** in the game, so the reward makes finishing roughly twice as
attractive relative to losing somebody as the economy does. That compounds with
the retreat bias above.

**No constant was changed.** Gold is the currency the TOWN trades in and the
reward's job is to order in-combat decisions; the two need not share a scale for
the ordering to be right. What the table establishes is that a single constant
cannot express a quantity the game varies this much, and that the one chosen is
at an extreme. The cheap testable version is to scale the death term by the
hero's own investment, which the simulator already knows since it sets gear
ranks per tier. Two seeds, paired, or it is not evidence.

Two numbers in the tool will rot silently and both are pinned by tests:
`self._reward_win = 8.0` is assigned in `__init__` and cannot be imported, and
the terminal-stress derivative divides by **200** (the ceiling) while
`END_STRESS_SCALE` in the same file is **100** (the Resolve Check) -- using the
wrong one halves every ratio above.

## THE PRE-TRAINING AUDIT: THE BRIDGE IS DOWN, AND SGD IS NOT THE BOTTLENECK (2026-09-02)

Fran: *"Before training i feel it would be a good moment to audit the proyect,
refactor it and make it more efficient for training if possible"*. Measured
first, in every case, because the standing notes turned out to be stale in both
directions.

### THE HEADLINE IS NOT PERFORMANCE: THE SHIPPED CHECKPOINT NO LONGER LOADS

    ValueError: Unexpected observation shape (1652,) for Box environment,
    please use (1636,) or (n_env, 1636) for the observation shape.

That is `bridge/advisor.py` on a captured save. The live bridge cannot make a
combat decision, `bat/combat_advise.bat` and `bat/combat_play.bat` are down,
and so is every eval against `models/dd_combat_ppo_full_expedition.zip`.

**The cause is a DATA fix, not a code change.** The enemy slot carries a
one-hot over the monster types the STORE contains -- `_skill_slot_indices`'s
own docstring says "the enemy-type one-hot width is a property of the loaded
store, so this cannot be a module constant either" -- and it sits in every
enemy rank. "THE DLC MONSTER TREES WERE NEVER READ", the same day, reached four
more types: `carpentry`, `ironwork`, `thatchery`, `cauldron`. Four types times
four ranks is **16 floats**, and the sequence env went 1636 -> 1652.

**Nothing caught it, and the reason generalises.** Every test that touches the
width DERIVES it from the env -- correct discipline for indices, and precisely
why none of them can see the width itself move. The suite was green throughout.
The CHANGELOG entry for the DLC fix does not mention the observation at all.
CLAUDE.md still said "only 1636 works with the current engine".

So the rule this file already carries for observation CONTENT --
"changing an observation-feeding function silently invalidates old
checkpoints" -- has a harder sibling: **a loader fix can change the observation
WIDTH with no edit in `env/` anywhere.** `tests/suite/test_obs_width_tripwire.py`
pins the type set and both absolute widths, and says in its docstring that
failing is the tripwire working: decide to invalidate the checkpoints, then
bump. A mods-on store legitimately differs, which is the same mechanism.

**It is fixed by the retrain and not before.** The next checkpoint trains at
1652. Until then the live bridge cannot play.

### SGD IS 49% OF WALL CLOCK, NOT 73%

`train.py`'s help text has said "SGD is ~73% of wall clock" since the flag was
added -- at batch **64**. At the shipped `--batch-size 256` it is **49%**,
measured by timing PPO's own two phases over two full updates at 12 envs:

| | collect | SGD | 12M projection |
|---|---|---|---|
| batch 256, 12 envs | 51% | 49% | 3.18 h |
| batch 256, 16 envs | 49% | 51% | 3.07 h |
| **batch 512, 12 envs** | 61% | **39%** | **2.69 h** |
| batch 1024, 12 envs | 67% | 33% | 2.39 h |

**Rollout and SGD are about half each, so neither side alone can save more
than half** -- which is the single most useful number for deciding what to
optimise, and it was pointing the wrong way in the docs.

`--batch-size 512` is the largest lever measured: **15% off a 12M run** for one
flag. NOT adopted here, because batch size changes the optimisation and not
just its speed -- 64 -> 256 was worth +1.6 SUCCESS on its own. It wants a
paired quality arm, like every other hyperparameter here. `--n-envs 16` is
worth 3.5% and is nearly free; 16 workers on 16 logical cores is where the
parallel half stops paying.

### DEAD WORK ON THE HOTTEST PATH: 10% OF AN ENV STEP, THROWN AWAY

`threat_forecast` computed `p_deaths_door` and `p_death` on every observation.
Those two features were appended to the observation on 2026-08-25 and
**reverted the same day** for being worth nothing measurable -- and computing
them survived the revert. That left `_forecast_kill_chance` and two survival
products running inside the single most expensive function on the hottest path
to fill two keys nothing reads. For over a week.

`threat_forecast(death_odds=False)` is what the observation asks for now. The
keys are OMITTED rather than zeroed, so a caller that wants them and forgets to
ask gets a `KeyError` rather than a plausible `0.0`; the default stays `True`,
so `tools/time_to_secure.py` and `tests/tools/test_ablate_forecast.py` are untouched.
The same pass hoisted a forecast sum the three party-level globals were taking
**twice** between them over a dict that cannot change in between.

| | before | after |
|---|---|---|
| env step | 602 us | **533 us** (1.13x) |
| `threat_forecast` | 164 us | **124 us** (1.32x) |
| rollout collection | 1,950 steps/s | **2,056 steps/s** |

**Byte-identical, and proven rather than asserted**: sha256 over 1800 steps of
observations, action masks and rewards across six seeds is unchanged
(`fc8af297…`). That harness is how any further work here should be checked.

Note how much smaller the end-to-end win is than the micro-benchmark: an 11.5%
cut to `env.step` is ~5% of collection and ~3% of the run, because
`collect_rollouts` is also the policy forward pass and the subprocess IPC. Quote
the end-to-end number.

### Two more stale measurements, corrected in place

* `_clamp01`'s docstring said it is called "~74x per env step (~297k times per
  4000 steps)". Re-counted: **354x per step, 1.42M per 4000 steps** -- 4.8x
  low, which strengthens the argument for the function rather than weakening
  it. A performance note is a measurement and goes stale like any other.
* CLAUDE.md's observation breakdown was three numbers out of date (hero slot
  191 -> 196, one-hot 9 -> 13, `GLOBAL_FEATURES` 14 -> 17) and gave the base
  width as 1605.

### What was MEASURED AND NOT ACTED ON

* **37.2% of the observation is constant** over 3,273 sampled observations, in
  280 runs. That is NOT waste and must not be trimmed on this evidence: the
  sample is eight seeds, the encoding is deliberately sparse (a payload slot
  for "applies burn" is zero for nineteen of twenty-one classes), and a wider
  comp sample lights more of it up. Recorded so nobody else chases it.
* **`engine/combat_engine.py` is 6,015 lines** and splitting it was considered
  and rejected: it has no functional benefit, and docs across this repo cite it
  by line number, so the churn would invalidate more than it improves.

## THE CORRIDOR IS A PLACE NOW, AND THE BANDAGE HAS A JOB (2026-09-02)

`rules.json` ships `dotHallwayStepRatio = 0.5` -- a DoT advances at half rate
per hallway tile -- and nothing read it. DoTs started crossing the combat
boundary earlier the same day, on the strength of that key; until this landed
they then **sat there costing nothing** until the next fight began, which left
the key's own premise unreachable one step further along.

**THE TILE COUNT IS MEASURED, NOT DERIVED, and the difference matters.**
`scripts/map_generator.darkest` gives `.base_corridor_number 10` and
`.spacing 4`, and `spacing` is the obvious candidate -- it is a grid stride
between room centres, not a tile count, and reading it as one would be a
convention wearing game data's clothes. `bridge/dungeon_map.py`'s `Area.tiles`
is the real thing off a real `persist.map.json`, and the capture corpus was
already on disk:

| tiles | corridors | |
|---|---|---|
| 3 | 1 | 2.1% |
| 4 | 2 | 4.2% |
| 5 | 24 | 50.0% |
| 6 | 12 | 25.0% |
| 7 | 6 | 12.5% |
| 8 | 2 | 4.2% |
| 9 | 1 | 2.1% |

48 corridors, seven distinct generated dungeons, **mean 5.625, median 5**.
`bat/play.log` agrees from a different direction: its `a fight started after N
step(s)` lines are the walk the actuator actually performed, running 1-11 with
a mean of 4.3 (n=23) -- lower because a fight can start part-way down a
corridor, which is the same quantity seen from the near end and brackets the
map figure from below. Two independent sources, one measured off the game's
saves and one off the bridge's own keystrokes.

`5.625 * 0.5` rounds to **3 ticks per boundary**.

**A CONSTANT, NOT A DRAW.** The sequence env is a list of combats with no map
between them, so there is no corridor to measure per boundary -- and drawing
one would consume `_sampler_rng` and put a different dungeon in front of the
two arms of every A/B this project runs. The expectation is what matters and a
constant delivers it exactly. Same discipline `.dot_irresistible` follows by
SKIPPING its roll rather than rolling against a zero resist; a test asserts
`rng.call_count` is unchanged by the walk.

### What it is actually worth, which is not what the raw rate suggests

The first measurement said 18.4% of boundaries carry a DoT, and taken alone
that reads as "this matters a lot". It does not, and the reason is the good
news: **a provisioned party pays nothing.** `_use_provisions_between_combats`
runs one block above the walk, and three bandages plus three antivenoms cover
every DoT a typical run brings to a boundary -- so the corridor finds nothing
to tick.

Emptying the pack, which is the tail of a long expedition, shows the mechanic:
over 12 long runs, **27% of boundaries carry a DoT and each costs about 5 HP.**

So the real effect is not a damage number. **It is that the bandage now has a
job it has never had in this simulator.** Before DoTs crossed the boundary the
cure was a pure no-op that spent a finite provision to prevent a bleed the next
`reset()` was about to clear for free -- strictly worse than doing nothing.
Before the corridor ticked, carrying the bleed still cost nothing. The item is
worth carrying for the first time, and `test_the_bandage_finally_prevents_
something` is the coherence check on the whole chain.

That also corrected a test written the same morning.
`test_the_cure_is_only_worth_anything_because_the_bleed_would_have_CARRIED`
asserted the carried bleed list was non-empty -- and a three-tick bleed is
exactly used up by a three-tick corridor, so the list is empty either way.
**It was passing for the wrong reason**: an untreated bleed running its course
looks identical to a cure working, if emptiness is what you check. It measures
the HP cost now, which is the thing the cure actually prevents.

### Three choices inside it, each of which could have been got wrong

* **A retreat walks no corridor.** `completed=False` means the party broke off
  and is standing in the corridor it came FROM. The same gate that denies it
  the meal and the campfire, for the same reason: fleeing must never become a
  way to BUY anything.
* **Bleed and blight only.** Burn is a decaying stack rather than a tick budget
  -- `_tick_burn`'s own comment says there is nothing to decrement -- and it
  ticks at the end of a TURN, which a corridor has none of. Horror is left
  because stress crossing 100 owes a Resolve Check and `_apply_stress` is what
  fires one; ticking it here would apply the stress and skip the check, which
  is a worse error than not ticking it.
* **Nobody dies in a corridor.** A hero emptied on the walk arrives at the next
  fight on Death's Door at 0 HP -- exactly what `_handle_deaths_door` does for
  a hero not already on it -- but the deathblow roll a hero already on the door
  would face is NOT taken. A draw between combats moves the RNG stream, and a
  death arriving where nothing can respond to it is what the starvation clamp
  three lines above already refuses in Fran's own terms. The consequence is
  carried into the next fight, where the engine can resolve it properly.

### AND THE SAME OBJECT SHIPS HALLWAY STRESS, WHICH NOTHING READS

Found while looking for the DoT ratio, in the same `rules.json` block:
`hallway_per_tile_stress_damage_fwd 2.0` at `chance_fwd 0.3`, and 5.0 at 0.55
`backing_up`. `DECOMP.md` already lists "how these are applied" as an open
question; the corridor makes them implementable, and they are **not
implemented**, because the reading differs by **4x** depending on who takes the
hit and no shipped key says.

Party-wide is 0.6 stress per hero per tile, ~50 over a long run. One hero per
tile is 0.15, ~13. Fran's `bat/play.log` bounds it: **367 points of stress rise
across 604 hero-steps = 0.608 per hero-step**, which matches party-wide almost
exactly -- and that is precisely why it cannot be quoted as confirmation. That
bucket also contains the fight that followed, and 70.5% of all observed stress
rise happens inside fights, so party-wide would consume the entire boundary
bucket and leave nothing for combat that is demonstrably in it. **0.6 is an
upper bound the data cannot exceed, not a measurement.**

It is worth deciding rather than dropping, because it composes with machinery
that already exists and is validated: the darkness table's
`stress_chance_increase` (0/5/10/25/35 points) and `stress_damage_increase`
(0/10/20/30/40 percent) are already parsed and already applied to combat stress
as `_stress_chance_bonus` / `_stress_damage_mult`. At pitch black a tile would
be 65% for 2.8 -- three times the lit rate, which is the mechanic players
describe. And it is the leading candidate for the part of Fran's "why is
real-play stress so much higher" that the underleveled penalty does not
explain. `ASSUMPTIONS.md` carries both readings and the falsifier: one live
capture walking a known corridor at a known torch level settles it.

## THE PARTY IS HANDED PROVISIONS AND THE PLAN WAS BUYING THEM AGAIN (2026-09-02)

`campaign/provision/provision.json` carries a list nothing read:
`raid_starting_hero_class_item_lists`. Eight classes bring a provision into
every raid, free -- **houndmaster 2 dog treats, plague doctor 1 antivenom,
grave robber 1 shovel, arbalest 1 bandage, crusader 1 holy water, leper 1
medicinal herbs, jester 1 medicinal herbs, antiquarian 1 skeleton key** --
which is darkestdungeon.wiki.gg's list exactly, parsed rather than transcribed.

PLAYBOOK 1.5's table says what to **carry**, and the grant is part of what is
carried, so `bridge/embarkplan.py` was buying it twice. On the four-hero
parties the planner actually produces that is **150 to 500 gold a week** -- a
Crusader/Leper/Houndmaster/Arbalest party takes a Ruins/short basket from 3550
to 3050 with the same pack at the end of it.

`data/provisions.granted(hero_classes, length)` reads both shipped lists.
Three rules:

* **It is PER HERO, not per party.** Two Houndmasters bring four Dog Treats, a
  Leper and a Jester bring two lots of herbs. `hero_classes` is a sequence and
  the docstring says so, because passing a set would silently undercount
  exactly the party that benefits most.
* **The firewood comes from the other list**,
  `raid_starting_length_inventory_item_lists[length]` -- 0 / 1 / 2 for the
  three lengths embarked on here, which is `FIREWOOD_PER_BAND` derived a second
  way from a second file, and a test pins that the two agree.
* **`basket(..., party=())` defaults to no party and is byte-identical**, so
  every existing caller is untouched -- the discipline the five training knobs
  follow. A test asserts equality across all four zones, three lengths and both
  levels.

**The order is a real constraint, not an oversight.** The grant is a property
of the party and the party is chosen out of what the reservation leaves, so
`plan_embark` reserves the **un-discounted** basket first and applies the grant
in `_basket_for` once seats exist. That direction is the safe one: the skill
budget can only turn out larger than promised. The plan's notes name what was
skipped, because a silent discount and a basket bug look the same from outside.

### `test_heirlooms.py` was pinning a fact that expired

`test_no_BUILDING_upgrade_appears_and_the_wallet_says_why` asserted the live
save contained **no** building upgrade -- true of a Week 22 snapshot, and false
the moment the bridge bought `stage_coach.numrecruits.a` for real. The save now
carries six across all three buildings (`numrecruits` a and b, `rostersize` a,
`blacksmith.weapon` a, `blacksmith.armour` a, `guild.skill_levels` a).

The defect was pinning a live save's CONTENT in a file that tests a READER.
What replaces it survives a campaign progressing and is not a tautology:
`read_purchased` derives its answer from the node list it is given, so
"everything it returns is real" is true by construction -- but WHICH TREES it
found comes from matching the save's raw hashed `tree_id`s, so the test hashes
the ids independently and requires the two agree, plus that no tree reports
more ranks than it ships. Both sides are empty on a fresh slot, which is what
keeps it honest across an `overnight.py` reset.

**And it is a live finding for the embark plan**: `blacksmith.weapon a` is
bought, so the gear-rank prerequisite that made this week "skills only" no
longer holds. The resolve-level gate still does for ten of the twelve heroes;
whether gear is now worth buying is Fran's call, not the planner's.

## PROVISIONS CANNOT BE USED IN BATTLE, AND THE SIMULATOR OFFERED FOUR (2026-09-02)

Fran: *"Another thing our model really lacks on the bat file is the usage of
bandages, antivenom and other provisions"* -- and the investigation inverted the
premise.

**The game forbids it, in as many words.**
`localization/miscellaneous.string_table.xml` ships
`cant_use_items_in_battle` -- *"Can't use items during battle"* -- twice, once
plainly and once as `str_user_information_cant_use_items_in_battle`, the prefix
DD uses for a refusal shown to the player, and translated ("Objets
inutilisables en combat"). Provisions are used in a hallway or a cleared room,
between fights.

So `IN_COMBAT_PROVISION_IDS` offered the policy **four free, turn-preserving
actions a real party can never take** -- and `bridge/run.py` was right all
along to report no button for them, because there is no button. The bridge was
not missing a capability; it was refusing an impossible one.

**The waste this file already recorded is the tell, in hindsight.** The engine's
own comment says 82% of the trained policy's provision uses were wasted, 274 of
278 laudanums drunk with no Horror. A policy will happily learn to spam a free
action. It cannot learn that the action does not exist.

### They moved to where the game puts them

`ExpeditionSequenceEnv._use_provisions_between_combats` runs at the same
between-rooms moment that already models eating and the hunger check: bandage a
bleed, antivenom a blight, laudanum a Horror, each gated on the condition it
treats. That gate survives the move for the reason it was added -- a provision
is finite and has to last 8-18 combats, and its cost is invisible at the moment
of use.

**`holy_water` is deliberately absent.** It is a proactive buff rather than a
cure, so there is no condition to gate it on; "use it when it would help" has no
honest reading here, and applying it after every fight would spend the stock on
corridors rather than on the fight it is meant to open. Recorded rather than
guessed, like every other two-reading key.

### THE CURE WAS A NO-OP FOR AN HOUR, AND ONE SHIPPED NUMBER FIXED IT

Moving the cure out of combat and stopping there would have been worse than
leaving it alone. `CarryOverHeroState` did not carry DoTs -- its docstring said
"skill buffs/debuffs expire in the corridor after combat" and quietly filed
bleeds under the same heading -- so a bandage in the corridor cured a bleed the
next `reset()` was about to clear **for free**. The method spent a finite
provision to prevent nothing, on every hero, after every fight.

`shared/rules.json` settles it in one key: **`dotHallwayStepRatio = 0.5`**. A
DoT keeps ticking as the party WALKS, at half rate per hallway tile. A rate for
ticking a DoT in a corridor is only meaningful if the DoT gets to the corridor,
so bleeds, blights, stress-DoTs and burn now cross the boundary, and the
bandage prevents something real. It is also the whole reason the item exists:
you carry bandages because a bleed follows you out of the room.

**The hallway TICK is deliberately not modelled** -- the sequence env is a list
of combats with no tiles between them, and inventing a tile count would be a
convention wearing game data's clothes. The simulator is now gentler than the
game by half a hallway's ticks per room, stated in `ASSUMPTIONS.md` rather than
hidden. Burn carries on the same evidence: the ratio names no DoT type, and
singling it out would need an argument nothing supplies.

Two rules that must not be regressed. The DoTs are **copied, never aliased** --
the env holds one carry record per hero for the whole expedition, and handing
the new `CombatantState` the same `DotInstance` would let the next combat's
first tick rewrite what the previous room ended with. And a carried DoT can
legitimately have spent one tick by the time `reset()` returns, because
`reset()` auto-resolves up to the first hero turn; a test asserting an exact
`remaining_ticks` there is asserting the initiative roll.

**Measured, so it is not asserted: 18.4% of combat boundaries have a hero
holding a DoT** (9 of 49, twelve expeditions of random legal play, full pack).
That is a floor rather than an estimate -- random play both takes more hits and
kills more slowly than a policy -- but it is enough to say the change is not
cosmetic: a bandage now prevents something on roughly one room transition in
five, where before it prevented something on none of them.

**And the provisions are no longer gated on `completed`.** The meal and the
campfire are recoveries the game grants for clearing a room. Using an item is a
player action, and a party that fled is standing in a corridor with the same
pack and the same bleed -- about to re-take the encounter at full strength,
which is the worst possible moment to still be bleeding.

### The Dog Treats exception, and why nothing had to be undone

darkestdungeon.wiki.gg says Dog Treats *"can be consumed in battle … Only
usable by a Houndmaster"* -- which reads as a flat contradiction of the entry
above, so it was checked before anything else.

Six shipped effects carry **`.item 1 .target "performer"`**: `bandage`
(`.cure_bleed`), `antivenom` (`.cure_poison`), `medicinal_herbs`
(`.clear_debuff`), `laudanum` (`.clearDotStress`), `holy_water` (four resist
buffs) and `dog_treats` (`.duration 3 .attack_rating_add 15%
.damage_low_multiply 50% .damage_high_multiply 50%` -- the wiki's numbers
exactly). `.item 1` marks "delivered by consuming an inventory item"; it says
nothing about **when**.

The verdict is that the wiki **corroborates** the change. It calls out
in-battle use for Dog Treats and for **no other provision** -- so the four the
simulator offered are unusable by the wiki's own account too, and the single
exception it names is an item this project does not model at all: `dog_treats`
costs 0 gold, appears in no `default_store_inventory_item_lists` row, is
granted only to a Houndmaster by `raid_starting_hero_class_item_lists`, and has
never been in `PROVISION_ACTION_ORDER`. No shipped file expresses the exception
either -- the supply row carries no in-battle flag, no Houndmaster skill
references the item, and the only data-side consumption path is the
`consume_item` act-out, which ships at **chance 0 in all thirteen base traits**
and chance 3 in Color of Madness's `color_madness` alone.

**A negative from `tools/pe_anchors.py` was nearly quoted here and is worthless
for this class of string.** It reports `cant_use_items_in_battle` absent from
`Darkest.exe` -- and it reports `cant_unequip_trinket_in_battle` absent too,
which is an indisputably real restriction. Every localization id tested came
back absent, so the tool does not reach them. Its own rule already says
"absent" is only evidence for a key the shipped data contains and the game
parses; a UI string is neither.

### `has_item_id` is an unread buff `rule_type`, with zero exposure today

Ten Color of Madness trinket buffs gate on **carrying** an item --
`TRINKET_com_hnd_huskfang_whistle_bleed_chance_B50_w_biscuit` wants
`dog_treats`, others want holy water, laudanum, herbs, a bandage.
`_is_conditional_rule_active` grants any `rule_type` it has no branch for, so
all ten currently fire unconditionally -- the same shape as the `lightbelow`
finding, which was worth 1.70 wrongly-carried buffs per combat. This one is
worth nothing yet: every carrier is a CoM trinket and no curated comp equips
one. Filed in `ASSUMPTIONS.md` rather than wired, so the day a comp does, the
count is already written down.

### The action indices did not move

`IN_COMBAT_PROVISIONS_ALLOWED = False` is a flag rather than deleted code, so
the old behaviour is one edit away if the reading is ever falsified and the
reason lives beside it. `PROVISION_ACTION_ORDER` is still `[32..35]` and
`ACTION_SPACE_SIZE` is still 39, so **every archived checkpoint still loads** --
the actions are simply never legal, exactly as retreat is never legal on a
surprised opening round.

### Five tests asserted the old mechanic, and the suite caught a sixth thing

`tests/env/test_provisions_combat.py` was written around the invented behaviour, so
its subject changed rather than its assertions being loosened: it now pins that
no provision is ever offered in battle, that the game string exists (recorded in
a test and not only in a comment, because this reverses a mechanic the simulator
carried for months), and that every condition gate still works at the new site.

And `test_suite_hygiene.py` caught the rewrite putting new tests BELOW the
file's `__main__` runner -- which would have made `python tests/test_provisions_
combat.py` silently run four fewer tests than pytest does. Exactly the failure
that test exists for, on the day it was needed.


## CAMPING HAD NO FOOD PHASE (2026-09-02)

Fran: *"Lets model the camping for training properly, not just that, this just
makes sense for medium and long dungeons"*.

`resolve_camp` resolved the 12-point skill phase and the 33% ambush and
**skipped the meal entirely** -- so every medium and long expedition camped
without eating, which is not a thing a player has ever done.

**And it is shipped data, not a convention.** `rules.json meals_table`:

| type | rations_per | healing | stress |
|---|---|---|---|
| none | 0.0 | **-0.20** | **+15** |
| half | 0.5 | 0 | 0 |
| full | 1.0 | +0.10 | 0 |
| feast | 2.0 | **+0.25** | **-10** |

**`rations_per` is PER LIVING HERO**, which is what makes the wiki's *"food
costs reduce by one-quarter per dead party member"* fall out rather than
needing a rule: a feast is 2 x 4 = 8 food for a full party and 2 x 3 = 6 for a
party of three. No arithmetic was invented anywhere in this.

### Why the meal is a DECISION and not a top-up

**A feast is the only stress relief in the game that costs no respite points.**
8 food buys -10 stress on every living hero *and* 25% of their max HP, while
the entire 12-point skill budget stays free for everything else. On PLAYBOOK
0.1's hierarchy -- deaths, then stress, then HP -- that is close to the best
purchase in a raid, and the simulator could not see it at all.

`choose_meal` takes the richest meal the party can afford AND use:

* **never starve when anything is affordable.** `none` is -20% max HP and +15
  stress on every hero, four times the damage a `full` heals and more stress
  than a campfire usually removes. Two food is never the worse trade.
* **do not gorge a healthy, calm party.** A feast there is six food spent on
  nothing, and food is what the hunger check comes back for. `half` costs two
  and does nothing, which is exactly right.
* the two thresholds -- under 90% HP, or 10+ stress -- are the one declared
  convention, and they are the same reading `EAT_TOPUP_FRACTION` already makes
  for eating between rooms.

**The food phase runs BEFORE the skill phase**, which is the real game's order
-- the Shieldbreaker's nightmare bark fires *"after the party has eaten, but
before Camping Skills have been used"* -- and it matters here for the same
reason it matters there: the skill phase's value function reads HP and stress,
so feeding first is what stops it spending respite points healing damage the
meal was about to remove.

### `food=None` and `food=0` are different, and the default is None

They differ by 20% of every hero's max HP plus 15 stress. `None` means "no food
phase" and is the default, so every caller that predates it -- `bridge/camp.py`
and every test that camps a party directly -- gets exactly the behaviour it
had. `0` means an empty pack, and starves.

That distinction was not free: defaulting to `0` starved every existing camping
test, and eight of them went red at once. The tests were right and the default
was wrong.


## THE DLC MONSTER TREES WERE NEVER READ (2026-09-02)

Fran: *"Is there any other mechanic visible on the logs that is missing from
the game?"* -- so `bat/play.log` was scanned for everything the bridge saw and
could not account for. One thing dominates:

    [R1] cannot seed: unknown monster id(s): ['snake_cobra_A', 'snake_rattler_A', ...]

**Thirty times in one session**, on ordinary Cove and Crypts runs. Thirty
combat turns where the bridge could offer no advice at all, because
`HeroLoader._load_enemies` walked `game_dir / "monsters"` and nothing else.
Every DLC monster tree -- `dlc/*/monsters/` and
`dlc/*/features/*/monsters/` -- was invisible.

**These are not a special mode.** The Shieldbreaker ships mash files for all
four normal regions and two kinds of row:

* `flashback.<zone>.<tier>.mash.darkest` -- `named:` rows at `.chance 1`, gated
  on `.flashback_id shieldbreaker_N`. Fran: *"When camping, if there is a
  Shieldbreaker in the party, there is a 50% chance for a nightmare to occur"*,
  seven of them, each with a fixed composition and a minimum expedition level.
* `flashback.<zone>.conditional.<tier>.mash.darkest` -- `hall:` rows at
  `.chance .01` gated on `.completed_flashback_id shieldbreaker_6`. Fran: after
  the seventh, *"A group of snakes may randomly appear in corridor battles,
  replacing a normal enemy party. This can happen even if there is no
  Shieldbreaker in the party."*

So a party that has finished the nightmares meets snakes in every region, at
1% per corridor fight, forever -- and the simulator had never heard of them.

**246 -> 298 enemies and 191 -> 222 brains.** The gap was two loaders wide:
loading a monster without its AI gives it stats, skills and no way to choose
between them, and the DLC brains live in each DLC's own `raid/ai/`. Both are
merged **base-first, first-wins**, the rule every other DLC merge here follows
-- the Crimson Court re-ships several vanilla monsters, so that is not
theoretical.

### Two things the widening broke, both caught by existing tests

* **A CORPSE reached the enemy pool.** `_CORPSE_DIRS` was the exact pair
  `("corpse", "corpse_large")`, complete while only the base tree was read --
  and Color of Madness ships `corpse_crystal`, which walked straight into
  `store.enemies`, where an encounter table could have drawn four of them as a
  fight. Corpse directories are matched by PREFIX now, and `_load_corpses`
  walks the DLC trees too, so `corpse_crystal_A..D` land where they belong.
  `store.corpses` being a separate dict is exactly what made this catchable.
* **`arena.monster_brains.json` is not UTF-8.** The Arena PvP content ships
  encrypted, the same way its trinket file does -- which the loader already
  skipped rather than crashing on. Skipped here too; the other four DLC brain
  files load 31 + 3 + 22 + 2.

One count moved deliberately: `test_the_two_side_flags_are_not_complements`
went 25 -> 71 desires carrying both target-side flags. The property is
unchanged -- every one is still a brain's baseline flat `random_target` -- so
it is a bump, not a weakened assertion.

### What is still NOT modelled about a nightmare

The monsters and their AI load, so a snake fight can be seeded and advised.
The nightmare's own rules are not implemented, and are recorded rather than
guessed:

* the Shieldbreaker takes **20 Horror for 6 rounds**, unremovable by laudanum,
  and **cannot become Virtuous** during one;
* the light meter is **forced to 100** and cannot be changed;
* **stall penalties are disabled**, during a nightmare and during the random
  post-completion snake encounters;
* failure is **a Shieldbreaker reaching Death's Door**, not a wipe, and costs a
  quest-long +33% stress / -5 ACC debuff on her and +15% stress on the party;
* success grants a quest-long +3% CRIT / -33% stress received on her and -15%
  stress on the party, plus Aegis Scales and a trinket.

Camping is auto-resolved here and the nightmare fires at camp, so most of that
sits behind a mechanic this simulator deliberately does not let the policy
choose. Filed with the rest of the pre-retrain list.


## TWO MECHANICS THE ENGINE HAD AND THE ENVS COULD NOT REACH (2026-09-02)

Fran, accepting the cost up front: *"yes, wire the underleveled penalty, lets
try to fix the most as we can as the previous training will be outdated anyway
after fixing things, lets improve the fiability of the training to make it
better on the real game"*.

Both of these are the most expensive kind of gap: the code was already written
and correct, the tests passed, and the training distribution quietly excluded
something the real game does constantly.

**EVERY CHECKPOINT AND EVERY COMP SCORE FROM BEFORE THIS IS A DIFFERENT
RULER.** Checkpoints still load -- the observation is unchanged -- but the
distribution they were trained and measured on is not the one that exists now.

### 1. The underleveled-hero penalty

`UNDERLEVELED_STRESS_PER_LEVEL` (20) and
`UNDERLEVELED_STRESS_DMG_MULT_PER_LEVEL` (0.25) fire on
`quest_difficulty_level`, and **no env had ever passed it**.
`TIER_TO_QUEST_DIFFICULTY` = `{1: 1, 2: 1, 3: 3, 4: 3, 5: 5}` now does -- mash
tiers 1-2 / 3-4 / 5 are Apprentice / Veteran / Champion and DD's quest
difficulties for those are 1 / 3 / 5, the same three values
`town_state.Quest.difficulty` carries.

**Only the Apprentice bracket can be short**, and a test asserts that rather
than trusting it: `TIER_TO_RESOLVE_WEIGHTS` fields Veteran at resolve 3-4
against difficulty 3 and Champion at 5-6 against 5, so both are always at or
above the bar. Apprentice draws resolve 0 with weight 0.2, and that hero now
enters at **20 stress** and takes **+25% stress damage for the whole raid** --
which is the wiki's *"Resolve level 0 heroes will always have this stress
penalty on their first expedition"*, and exactly what Fran's own `play.log`
shows with 13 of 15 heroes entering at 20.

**The compounding half is the one that matters.** A flat +20 would leave the
eval understating by a constant; +25% widens the gap with every stress event,
which is what "much higher than i expected" looks like from the outside.

Two wiring details, both of which cost a debugging pass:

* **The opening combat of a sequence goes through the base env's `reset`**, on
  the explicit-scenario path -- so the difficulty has to travel in the OPTIONS
  dict, exactly as `zone_id` already did. An attribute set on the sequence env
  was wiped by `ExpeditionEnv.reset` before the engine saw it, and the first
  fight of every expedition was the one place the penalty did not apply.
* **`ExpeditionEnv.reset` reads the option rather than blanking the
  attribute**, so it still describes the run after `reset` returns instead of
  reading stale.

**An explicit scenario is unchanged unless it asks.** Every pinned-comp path --
`eval_expedition`, `bridge/rollout.py`, `tools/comp_ga.py` -- is byte-identical
without `quest_difficulty_level` in its options, the same rule the kit and
loadout knobs follow.

### 2. Torch 0, the band that could not be drawn

`rules.json` gives torch <= 0 its own darkness band -- **+35 stress chance,
+40% stress damage, +5 monster crit, +30% loot gold** -- and
`TORCH_LEVEL_DECILES` starts at **3.0**, so the sampler could not produce it.
Not rarely: never, in any training run or eval this project has taken.

The deciles are not wrong, they are **stale**. They were measured from 206
in-battle captures taken before `bridge/torchlight.py` existed, and that layer
now snuffs the torch to 0 on purpose for the loot. The corpus describes a
player who did not do the thing the bridge does.

`TORCH_SNUFFED_P = 4/30` is the measured replacement, from `bat/play.log` by
attributing each area entry to whether a fight actually followed it:

| band | old sampler | NEW sampler | real play, at the start of a fight |
|---|---|---|---|
| radiant | 63.9% | 61.5% | 63.3% |
| dim | 10.5% | 9.8% | 23.3% |
| dark | 15.5% | 15.2% | 0.0% |
| black | 10.1% | **0.0%** | 0.0% |
| pitch black | **0.0%** | **13.5%** | 13.3% |
| mean | 73.0 | 68.8 | 74.3 |

Three decisions inside that, each of which could have been got wrong:

* **A snuff is a POINT MASS, not an interpolated bin.** The band is `torch <=
  0`, and a continuous draw over [0, 25] lands ON zero with probability zero --
  so simply lowering the first decile to 0.0 would have looked like a fix and
  changed nothing. Somebody clicked the meter out; that is a discrete event.
* **The mass REPLACES the bottom decile rather than stacking on it.** The
  corpus minimum was 3, so its lowest bin is where a snuffed-and-partly-relit
  fight already landed; counting both double-counts the darkness and drags the
  mean down ten points. Real play agrees the bin should go -- of 30 logged
  fights, **none opened in `dark` or `black`**. A fight is either lit or
  snuffed; the in-between is where a party walks.
* **`dim` and `dark` are left on the 206-capture corpus.** Real play says 23.3%
  and 0.0% against the sampler's 9.8% and 15.2%, and n=30 carries about +-9pp
  per band -- re-fitting a decile table on thirty fights against two hundred
  captures would be trading a good estimate for a fashionable one. Recorded so
  a bigger `play.log` can revisit it; `tools/play_log_probe.py --distribution`
  is what re-measures.

### The Virtue Chance penalty, and the act-out chaining rule (same day)

Fran supplied the exact semantics, and they decode the one table that had
looked unimplementable:

    Heroes with a lower level than the dungeon they are in get a penalty of -5%
    Virtue Chance per level difference, except when the difference is 6 ...
    where the penalty is -33% ... Virtue Chance is always at least 1% and is
    capped at 95%.

**`effectiveDifficultyAfflictionOnsetModifiers` is indexed by LEVEL SHORTFALL,
not by quest difficulty**, and that one word is the whole reason it sat unread.
By difficulty it puts virtue at zero for every Champion party, which cannot be
right and is why it was recorded as a two-reading gap that morning. By
shortfall the shipped `[0, .05, .10, .15, .20, .25, .33]` is the rule to the
digit:

| shortfall | 0 | 1 | 2 | 3 | 4 | 5 | 6 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| virtue chance | 25% | 20% | 15% | 10% | 5% | 0% -> **1%** | -8% -> **1%** |

**And the clamp is the rules' own affliction bounds, inverted.** The previous
comment in `engine/stress.py` said `affliction_min_chance` and
`affliction_max_chance` were *"stated for the affliction chance, not this one,
so they are deliberately not reused here"* -- and 1 - 0.99 = **1%** and
1 - 0.05 = **95%** are exactly the documented floor and ceiling. Two sides of
one roll share one pair of bounds. That comment is now a correction rather than
a caveat.

Measured over 3,000 resolve checks per shortfall: 25.0 / 20.0 / 15.0 / 10.0 /
5.0 / 1.0 / 1.0 percent.

**`CombatantState.level_shortfall` is what carries it**, set at build time
beside the entry stress and the stress multiplier -- so one number drives all
three halves of being underlevelled, and it is impossible for them to disagree.

#### Act-outs cannot chain

    act-outs cannot happen two times in a row for the same hero, and two
    different heroes cannot have act-outs occur back-to-back

Not modelled. The existing guard only stopped **two rolls in one turn**; it
said nothing about consecutive turns, so an afflicted party could act out on
adjacent turns and again the round after. That is the difference between an
affliction being a serious problem and being a spiral -- the thing Fran rates
*"almost the same as a death"*.

Three cases, and the third is the one a naive fix gets wrong:

* two heroes on adjacent turn indices in one round;
* one hero on their own consecutive turns, which are a **round** apart rather
  than adjacent indices -- so it needs its own per-hero record;
* **across the round boundary**: the last hero of one round and the first of
  the next are back-to-back by any reading, so the record is deliberately NOT
  cleared when the turn order is rebuilt.

Checked BEFORE the roll, so a suppressed turn does not consume one -- "cannot
happen" rather than "happens and is discarded".

### An affliction lasts the raid, and ENDS at 0 stress (same day)

Fran:

    if a hero is afflicted, it'll remain afflicted for the whole dungeon unless
    his stress value reaches 0 again ... Afflictions can also be cured within a
    dungeon, by reducing the Afflicted hero's stress to 0 during an expedition.

**Persistence was already right.** `CarryOverHeroState` carries the trait id
between combats and restores it by id rather than re-rolling, so a broken hero
stays broken across the raid and any number of heroes can be broken at once.

**The CURE was missing.** Seven places reduce hero stress and not one looked at
the flags, so a hero healed to 0 kept the personality and its entire buff list
for the rest of the run. **The simulator was HARSHER than the game**, which is
the rarer direction and the reason it went unnoticed for so long: a fidelity
gap that makes things worse does not look like a bug in a win rate.

`_maybe_clear_overstress` is the cure, called from every in-combat stress
reduction and from `engine/camping.py` -- camping being where most of a raid's
stress healing actually happens, and it sits exactly on the combat boundary.
`_apply_carry_over` refuses to hand the trait back when `carry.stress <= 0`,
or the recovery would be undone the moment the next room started.

**And the buffs have to come off with it.** `TraitDefinition.buff_ids` is the
complete effect list -- the -15 resists and MAXHP-10 an affliction shares are
entries inside its own list -- so leaving them behind keeps a cured hero
permanently weakened, which is the same defect one layer down.

#### The integer stats do not round-trip, so they are snapshotted

Reversing a multiplicative buff through `int()` loses a point:

    MAXHP-10 on 61      int(61 * 0.90) = 54,  int(54 / 0.90) = 60
    Paranoid -25% DMG   int(10 * 0.75) =  7,  int(7  / 0.75) =  9

One point per break-and-recover cycle, and a hero can go through several in a
long raid. `CombatantState.overstress_stats` snapshots exactly the three stats
`combat_stat_multiply` handles as integers -- `max_hp`, `dmg_min`, `dmg_max` --
before the trait's buffs go on, and the cure restores them rather than
reversing. Every other stat a trait touches is a float and reverses cleanly to
within epsilon. HP itself is only re-capped, never raised: recovering from an
affliction is not a heal.

### What was deliberately NOT changed, and why

Three more gaps were on the pre-retrain list and none of them ships, because
each still has two readings and this repo does not ship guessed fixes. (A
fourth, `effectiveDifficultyAfflictionOnsetModifiers`, DID ship once Fran
supplied the index -- see above. The lesson is that "two readings" sometimes
means "nobody has said which", not "the data cannot say".)

* `stall_round_reset_threshold = 4` -- the counter resets after 4 clean rounds,
  or the penalty stops after 4 rounds of it.
* the virtue `block_effect` -- shipped at chance 1.0 on all five virtues, and
  "a virtuous hero blocks everything" cannot be the reading.
* `deaths_door_*_repeat` -- every subsequent hit at Death's Door, or the second
  entry to it.


## FRAN'S VALUE HIERARCHY, AND THE RULER THAT DOES NOT MATCH IT (2026-09-02)

He stated the objective function outright, which is the highest-value kind of
input this project gets:

1. **finish without deaths** -- *"an end game hero means many resources spent,
   so the value of the party alive and with the resources of the dungeon
   outvalues the stress of the party"*;
2. **low stress** -- *"stress carries outside the dungeon, making the hero
   unable of playing dungeons back to back as opposed to HP"*;
3. **HP last** -- *"hp doesnt carry outside the dungeon, the heroes enter full
   HP, hp matters only to not lose heroes, but if you outvalue hp to stress its
   a problem as the afflictions will defeat you in the end"*.

Everything else is instrumental: *"any kind of damage prevents enemy
stress/damage if they are defeated, same for stuns, but stuns only work for 1
turn, in exchange, they always go net positive in action economy"*. Damage and
control are the two ways of stopping the enemy acting, and a stun pays for
itself only because it almost always arrives attached to something else.

**Point (1) is already satisfied**: a dead hero scores zero for its whole share
of BOTH halves of `_run_quality`, and the denominator is the roster rather than
the survivors precisely so a policy cannot improve mean stress by letting the
stressed hero die.

**Points (2) and (3) are not.** `_run_quality` weights HP and stress **50/50**,
and `tools/comp_ga.py` has ranked on **2:1 stress-over-HP** since 2026-08-26 --
a call Fran made for the search. So the eval and the search have been using
different rulers for months, and his hierarchy says the search is the closer of
the two.

### What changed, and what deliberately did not

`QUALITY_WEIGHTS` and `QUALITY_ALT` are named constants now, `_run_quality`
takes a weighting, and **every band line prints the same runs re-scored at 1:2
beside the declared 50/50**. That costs no rerun at all, because
`RolloutResult` has always carried the two halves unweighted -- kept that way
for `comp_ga`, and it pays for itself here.

**The DEFAULT stays 50/50.** Changing it re-scores every measurement this repo
has ever taken -- comp scores, leaderboards, the shipped checkpoint's own table
-- so it is a new ruler rather than a fix, and it is Fran's call. Showing both
costs nothing and lets the decision be made on evidence instead of on the
argument.

Worked example of what the weights actually do:

    hp 1.00 calm 0.70   50/50  85.0   1:2  80.0    -5.0
    hp 0.50 calm 0.90   50/50  70.0   1:2  76.7    +6.7
    hp 1.00 calm 0.40   50/50  70.0   1:2  60.0   -10.0

The re-weighting is worth up to ten points on a stressed party, and it narrows
the gap between "came home hurt and calm" and "came home healthy and stressed"
without yet inverting it -- so 1:2 is a nudge in Fran's direction rather than
the full statement of his hierarchy. Whether it should go further is exactly
what the printed column exists to inform.


## WHY REAL-PLAY STRESS IS HIGHER, AND THE ANSWER I GOT WRONG FIRST (2026-09-02)

Fran: *"We did fine tune the model and got really low stress even on champion
dungeons, what is happening that on real play its much higher than i
expected?"*

### RETRACTED: it is not the torch

The first answer here was that `TORCH_LEVEL_DECILES` starts at 3.0 and so can
never draw the pitch-black band, while real play sat there 22.6% of the time --
the band that adds +35 stress chance, +40% stress damage and +5 monster crit.

**Fran rejected the measurement and he was right:** *"that distribution is not
that proper as is not the same the torch on combat than outside combat"*. The
sampler draws a torch level **per combat**; the 22.6% was pooled over every
`=== area ===` line in the log, which is mostly TRAVEL. Attributing each area
entry to whether a fight actually followed it separates them:

| band | simulator (per combat) | real, at the START OF A FIGHT (n=30) | real, areas with NO fight (n=110) |
|---|---|---|---|
| radiant | 63.9% | **63.3%** | 40.9% |
| dim | 10.5% | 23.3% | 17.3% |
| dark | 15.5% | 0.0% | 5.5% |
| black | 10.1% | 0.0% | 0.9% |
| pitch black | 0.0% | **13.3%** | 35.5% |
| mean torch | 73.0 | **74.3** | 50.9 |

**In-combat torch matches training almost exactly** -- 63.3% radiant against
63.9%, mean 74.3 against 73.0. The darkness is where the party WALKS, which is
the torch layer behaving exactly as designed: it snuffs at the killing blow and
relights before moving on, so the dark readings are the gap between those two
events. What survives of the original claim is small and honest: **4 of 30
fights (13.3%) did open at pitch black, a band the sampler reaches 0.0% of the
time.** Real, worth fixing eventually, and nowhere near enough to explain the
stress gap.

The lesson is the one this file keeps relearning: a distribution is only
comparable to another distribution of **the same quantity**, and "torch" is two
quantities.

### And it is not travel stress either

The obvious follow-up was that the simulator models no out-of-combat stress at
all -- no hallway, no curio, no trap. Measured on the log by tagging each
hero's stress rise with whether an area boundary fell between the two readings:

    within a fight, no area change:  876 points
    across an area boundary:         367 points  (and that bucket INCLUDES
                                                  the fight that followed)

**70.5% of all observed stress rise happens inside a fight**, and the 29.5% is
an upper bound on travel because it pools the next fight in with it. Combat
stress dominates, and the simulator models combat stress.

### What it IS, as far as the log can say

**The eval starts every hero at 0 stress. Real heroes start at 20.**

    ExpeditionSequenceEnv.reset() ->  man_at_arms 0, houndmaster 0,
                                      houndmaster 0, arbalest 0

    play.log, first reading per hero -> 13 of 15 at exactly 20,
                                        Jester 51, Crusader and Highwayman 0
                                        median 20, mean 19.4

and every large stress DROP in the log lands back on 20, so 20 is the floor
this campaign returns to rather than a one-off. Stress is a CAMPAIGN quantity
-- it persists between raids and is bought down in town -- and the eval's comps
carry none, because `build_comp_pool` comps have no stress field and nothing
puts one there.

So the two numbers are not the same measurement:

* the eval's `stress 16.8` on the long band is stress **ACCRUED, from zero, in
  one expedition**;
* real play is **carried stress plus accrual**, and 20 + ~17 lands on the log's
  median of 36 and mean of 37.1.

**Do not over-read that arithmetic** -- the log's 37 is a time-average over
every decision, not an end-of-raid value, and the last-seen readings are far
higher (Shieldbreaker 180, Arbalest 100, Bounty Hunter 95). The solid part is
the offset and its direction: **an eval that starts at 0 cannot report the
number a campaign hero lives at**, and a +20 floor is most of a Resolve Check
before the first monster acts.

### THE 20 IS THE UNDERLEVELED PENALTY, AND IT IS IMPLEMENTED BUT UNREACHABLE

Fran named it:

    20 starting stress is because they are lower level than the mission (lvl 0
    heroes against lvl 1 mission) ... 20 stress for every level of difference
    ... and will take more stress damage than normal (+25% stress for every
    level of difference) ... Resolve level 0 heroes will always have this
    stress penalty on their first expedition

That is not carried campaign stress, which is what the previous section
guessed. It is `engine/combat_engine.py`, and both halves are already there:

    UNDERLEVELED_STRESS_PER_LEVEL = 20.0
    UNDERLEVELED_STRESS_DMG_MULT_PER_LEVEL = 0.25

**And no env has ever passed the argument that fires them.** The penalty is
gated on `quest_difficulty_level`, which defaults to `None`; grepping
`engine/`, `env/`, `bridge/`, `train.py` and `eval_expedition.py` finds the
parameter in the engine's own signature and in one comment. That comment, in
`env/expedition_env.py`, describes Fran's situation as a hypothetical:

    Fielding resolve 0 is safe with respect to the underleveled-hero penalty:
    that fires on `quest_difficulty_level`, which the envs never pass ... If a
    caller ever starts passing it, revisit this -- a resolve-0 hero on a
    difficulty-1 quest would then take 20 stress before the first turn.

The caller that started passing it is the real game.

**The compounding half is the one that matters.** A +20 offset would leave the
eval understating stress by a constant; **+25% stress damage for the whole
raid** makes the gap widen with every stress event, which is what "much higher
than i expected" looks like from the outside. It also explains why the log's
worst readings (Shieldbreaker 180, Arbalest 100) run so far past a mean the
eval reports as 16.8.

`TIER_TO_RESOLVE_WEIGHTS` draws **20% of Apprentice-bracket heroes at resolve
0** under `--resolve-sampling`, so the training distribution already contains
exactly the party this penalty is for -- and then never charges it.

**Fixing it is a training-distribution change**, the same status as the
resolve-level correction earlier today: passing the quest difficulty makes
