every Apprentice run with a resolve-0 hero harder than it was, so comp scores
and evals either side are different rulers. `docs/NEXT.md` carries it with the
arithmetic.

## AN AFFLICTION COSTS 0.15 IN THE REWARD AND NOTHING IN THE RULER (2026-09-02)

Fran, on the same thread:

    usually a hero on almost 100 stress goes to affliction/virtue and that
    complicates things too much (an afflicted hero its really bad almost the
    same as a death as it increases all the overall stress, and you only have a
    25% of virtue chance)

**The 25% is right and already correct here.** `affliction_critical_success_
chance` is 0.25 and `engine/stress.py` rolls it independently -- its docstring
records fixing a nested-roll version that gave P(virtue) = 0.75 x 0.25 =
18.75%.

What his sentence exposes is the pricing, not the mechanic:

* **The reward prices an affliction at `W_AFFLICTED = 0.15`**, inside Phi,
  against `W_STRESS = 0.50` and `REWARD_HERO_DEATH = -50.0`. If an afflicted
  hero really is "almost the same as a death", 0.15 of a potential term is
  three orders off, and it is a *potential* term, so it cannot change the
  optimal policy at all -- only the credit timing.
* **`_run_quality` does not count afflictions at all.** The ruler this whole
  project optimises and selects checkpoints against is *"per hero: 0 if dead,
  else half for HP and half for stress"*. A party that comes home intact but
  afflicted scores the same as one that comes home calm at the same stress
  number. Every comp score, every leaderboard and every checkpoint comparison
  in this repo is blind to the outcome Fran rates just below a death.

Two shipped mechanics are also unmodelled, both found looking for the above:

* **`effectiveDifficultyAfflictionOnsetModifiers`** `[0, 0.05, 0.1, 0.15, 0.2,
  0.25, 0.33]` -- the onset chance rises with quest difficulty and nothing in
  Python reads the key, so a Champion run afflicts at the Apprentice rate.
* **The history weights** Fran quotes -- `base_affliction_history_WF 4`,
  `affliction_history_increment [0, 2, 3, 4]`, `base_virtue_history_WF 4`.
  These bias WHICH trait a hero draws, and they are per-hero and persistent
  across raids, so they are genuinely out of scope for a per-expedition
  simulator. Recorded rather than implemented -- but the increments differing
  (2, 3, 4 for afflictions against Fran's +1 for virtues) is why a hero who has
  been afflicted before is likelier to be afflicted again, which is the
  campaign-level death spiral the reward cannot see.


## THE REAL-PLAY CORPUS WAS ALREADY ON DISK (2026-09-02)

The team-play ruler shipped with a caveat it could not remove from the inside:
it plays curated comps at a fixed tier, while Fran's impression came from
driving the bridge through his own campaign -- *"may be that the comps that i'm
using are super low level or with sub-optimized loadouts"*. Two distributions,
and no way to tell which one the disagreement belonged to.

Fran: *"i did run several dungeons with the bat, so you could check those if
combat_play.bat leaves any kind of log"*. It does. `bat/play.log` is **7,823
lines** and carries, per decision, the round, the actor, the chosen skill, the
target and **its current HP**, and the runners-up with theirs:

    [20:58:03] [R2] Plague Doctor (rank 3, 22/22 hp, 17 stress)
      -> INCISION on rank 2 (Cultist Acolyte, 5 hp)   79%
         alt: EMBOLDENING VAPOURS on rank 2 (Occultist)  14%

That is the whole input the ruler needs, with no simulator anywhere -- the same
virtue `tools/agreement.py` has, on a corpus recorded during serious play
rather than during bridge testing.

### `tools/play_log_probe.py`, on 263 executed decisions across 8 classes

| metric | real play | simulator (`team_probe`) |
|---|---|---|
| follow-up attacks repeating this round's target | **+8.1pp** vs chance | +7.3pp |
| decisions leaving a weaker enemy standing | **-2.9pp** vs chance | -20.0pp |
| control taken when it was on offer | **-10.0pp** below its share | -4.8pp |

**FOCUS replicates almost exactly**, on a different distribution and a
different instrument: +8.1 against +7.3. The policy converges its fire in
Fran's own dungeons too, so *"each hero plays its own game"* is refuted on both
rulers rather than on one.

**CONTROL is twice as bad in real play** -- 10.0pp below its share of the
shortlist against 4.8pp in the simulator. *"It infravalues the more support
skills"* is the half of his review that survives, and it is WORSE where he saw
it than where the simulator measures it.

**FINISH is barely better than chance live** (-2.9pp) against a decisive -20.0
in the simulator. Read this one carefully: the two baselines are not the same
quantity. The simulator compares against every legal target; the log only
prints the policy's own shortlist, so this is a comparison against its own top
choices. A weaker bar and a smaller gap are consistent with each other, and the
honest reading is that the log cannot resolve FINISH, not that the policy got
worse.

### Two silent parser bugs, both caught by a FIXTURE rather than by the log

Neither would have raised. Both would have reported a smaller, wronger corpus.

* **The convergence window is a ROUND, and several heroes act inside one.** The
  first version reset it whenever `round <= last_round`, which is true for
  every hero after the first in the same round -- so the set of already-struck
  enemies was always empty and **FOCUS silently graded n=0** while the other
  two metrics reported normally. Two heroes both acting in R1 is precisely the
  case being measured.
* **`(AoE)` broke the target capture.** An AoE line reads `on rank 2 (Brigand
  Fusilier, 20 hp) (AoE)  27%`, and a lazy `\((.+?)\)` anchored on the
  percentage swallows `) (AoE` into the name -- so the HP stops parsing, the
  entry reads as ally-directed, and **every AoE alternative vanishes from the
  reachable set**. Stopping the capture at the first `)` fixes it, and it moved
  the real numbers: FOCUS n 89 -> 107, FINISH n 138 -> 157.

The fixture in `tests/tools/test_play_log_probe.py` carries an AoE line and two
heroes in one round for exactly these reasons. The real log did not catch
either, which is the argument for having the fixture at all.


## "EACH HERO PLAYS ITS OWN GAME" IS NOT ABOUT TARGETING (2026-09-02)

Fran, after watching the actuator play real dungeons:

    Each hero plays its own game rather than thinking like a team it seems,
    sometimes it feels that each hero targets its own individual objective
    rather than playing with the whole build

Nothing here measured that. `tools/style_probe.py` grades WHICH SKILL against
the expert tier list and says so in its own docstring -- *"the ruler grades
WHICH SKILL and never WHICH TARGET"* -- and team play is almost entirely about
the target. `tools/team_probe.py` is that missing half.

**It refutes the hypothesis, on two of three metrics.** Shipped checkpoint, 30
comps x 4 episodes, tier 3 long x15:

| metric | policy | uniform legal pick | gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| follow-up attacks that hit an enemy already struck this round | **46.7%** | 39.3% | **+7.3pp** more focused |
| decisions with a hurt enemy in reach that hit a HEALTHIER one | **32.4%** | 52.4% | **-20.0pp** better at finishing |
| turns that took a control skill when one was on the bar | **24.8%** | 29.5% of legal actions were control | **-4.8pp** under its share |

n = 3642 / 1842 / 8503. On both target-selection measures the policy beats
chance, and it beats it decisively on finishing wounded enemies. Whatever "each
hero plays its own game" is, **it is not that the heroes fail to converge fire
or leave kills on the table.**

What survives is the other half of his review -- *"it outvalues just raw
numbers (high damage/heal actions) and infravalues the more support skills"* --
and it lands on the SKILL, not the target. Control is taken 4.8pp below its own
share of the bar here, and `style_probe`'s passed-over list is the stronger form
of the same finding: Inspiring Cry 568 times, Open Vein 754, If It Bleeds 832,
Leper's Intimidate -- an **S-tier** skill -- 465.

**Between-comp SD is 9.7pp on focus, range -17.0 to +32.4.** So target
selection does vary by comp, which is evidence AGAINST the "one plan" reading
on this axis specifically. `style_probe`'s finding that within-category SKILL
choice is about as good as chance at every sustain level stands unchallenged;
the two rulers are measuring different halves and disagreeing is what tells
them apart.

### Two bugs, both of which produced a plausible number instead of an error

**`Action.target_id` is a RANK, not a combatant id.** Enemy ids run from 100
and ranks from 0, so matching them against each other recorded nothing at all
-- the first run reported only CONTROL and every attack counter sat at zero.
Resolution goes through `occupied_positions`, not `.position`, because a
size-2+ monster is offered from either of its ranks.

**A per-ROUND focus baseline is biased by the thing it measures, and
correcting it FLIPPED THE SIGN.** The first version counted distinct enemies
per round against a balls-in-bins expectation, and read *"more spread than
chance"* -- which would have confirmed Fran's intuition and been wrong. A
focused party KILLS something, and every attack after that is forced onto a
different target, so the round-level count rewards exactly the behaviour it is
trying to detect the absence of.

Counting **per decision** removes it: at each attack, the chance a uniform
legal pick would have repeated is `|already hit and still reachable| /
|reachable|`, summed over the decisions actually graded. A dead enemy is not in
`reachable`, so it can neither be repeated nor expected. Same number, opposite
conclusion: **+7.3pp more focused**.

Every one of the three rates carries its own baseline for the same reason. A
bare "32.4% left a hurt enemy standing" has no scale -- on a board where three
of four reachable enemies are healthy, picking at random leaves one standing
most of the time and 32% is good play.

### It is a RULER

Same standing rule as `tools/check_class_play.py`, `tools/skill_tiers.py` and
`tools/style_probe.py`: **nothing it measures may become a reward term, an
action mask or a training target.** A policy fitted to a focus-fire target is a
scripted player, and target selection is precisely where a scripted player
stops transferring to a class it has never seen.
`tests/tools/test_team_probe.py` walks `engine/`, `env/`, `data/`, `bridge/` and
`train.py` and fails if any of them imports it.

### The caveat that belongs with any quote of these numbers

This is the SIMULATOR at tier 3 with curated loadouts. Fran was watching the
live bridge in his own campaign, and said so himself -- *"may be that the comps
that i'm using are super low level or with sub-optimized loadouts"*. That is a
different distribution from the one measured here, and the disagreement between
his impression and this table may be the distribution rather than the ruler.
The way to close it is `tools/agreement.py` against a corpus recorded during
serious play, which does not exist yet.


## THE SNUFF WAS A QUARTER OF A SNUFF, AND THE LOG SAID IT WAS OUT (2026-09-02)

Fran, after testing the actuator in a dungeon:

    Torch manipulation is not working properly, its not using ctrl+shift+click
    on the same time so it just lowers 25 torch with shift click each click, so
    either we go 4 clicks to lower it or do something else

The gesture the bridge sends is right on paper. `_mods` holds SHIFT and CTRL
down together across the click and releases both in a `finally`, and the game
documents both gestures on that widget -- `str_snuff_torch_tip` is
SHIFT+CTRL+CLICK for the full snuff, `str_reduce_torch_tip` is SHIFT+CLICK for
one 25-point step. What Fran saw is the SECOND one, which means CTRL is not
reaching SDL through `SendInput` on this setup.

**The defect worth fixing is not the modifier, it is that nothing checked.**
`persist.raid.json` carries `torchlight`, so this is one of the few actuations
in the whole bridge that can be acknowledged outright -- and it was the one
being taken on trust, inside a bundled plan, with the log reporting a snuff
that had removed a quarter of the torch.

### The snuff is executed and verified BEFORE the turn is planned

`_with_snuff` prefixed the click to the attack so both went out as one
`InputPlan`. That preserved the one ordering that is not negotiable -- the loot
roll is taken on the kill, so snuffing after the blow is too late -- and it
made the snuff impossible to acknowledge, because there is nowhere to read the
save between two steps of one plan.

`ActRunner._snuff_torch` now runs first, and the order is preserved because it
runs first. It sends the documented one-click gesture, **reads the torch
back**, and steps the remainder down with `plan_reduce_torch` (SHIFT only) when
it did not take -- re-reading after each click, so a meter that stops moving
stops the loop rather than feeding it. Bounded at `TORCH_MAX_CLICKS = 6`
against the four a 100 -> 0 needs.

Three properties, each a test:

* a snuff that lands sends **exactly one click** -- the fallback costs nothing
  when the gesture works, which is what makes it safe to keep trying first;
* a snuff that only dropped a step is finished by SHIFT-clicks, and the first
  click carries `{SHIFT, CTRL}` while every later one carries `{SHIFT}`;
* **the snuff still comes before the swing**, asserted on the execution order
  rather than on the plan shape, because the plan shape is exactly what
  changed.

A failed snuff is reported and **the turn is still played**. A snuff that
swallowed the attack would forfeit the very kill it exists to sweeten.

### What this does not do yet

Fran's torch POLICY is a bigger ask and is not built:

    we should also stay at +25 torch most of the time outside of curio/rewards
    interaction, for example, we should never start a combat at 0 torch, but we
    should try to chain most curio possible at 0 torch

`bridge/torchlight.py` decides one question -- shut down for this fight's loot
roll, or hold -- and every branch of it is about a combat. A travel policy
(a floor while walking, snuff for a curio, relight before a fight) is a
different decision with different inputs, and the extreme version he describes
(clear the combats, then backtrack at 0) needs scouting data the bridge does
not use. Recorded in `docs/NEXT.md` rather than guessed at.


## "RANDOMLY USING A INSTEAD OF D" AND "MAPPING IS BUGGED" ARE ONE BUG (2026-09-02)

Fran reported them as two items. They are the same defect, and the corpus says
why.

### `Walk.heading` reads the direction off the tile ORDER, and the order runs both ways

    D advances through the tile order, so a party that entered at tile 0 goes
    deeper on D and one that entered at the far end goes deeper on A.

Measured over all 399 map captures, 48 distinct corridors:

| tile order vs `mappos` | corridors |
|---|---|
| x ascending | 16 |
| x descending | 9 |
| y ascending | 16 |
| y descending | 7 |
| **NOT monotonic** | **0** |

So the tiles ARE stored in geometric order along the corridor -- and that order
runs **both ways**, 32 one way against 16 the other. Worse, **23 of the 48 are
vertical** (constant x), where map coordinates carry no screen left/right at
all. A rule that maps "entered at tile 0" onto "press D" is therefore right
about half the time, which is exactly what "randomly using A instead of D"
looks like from the keyboard.

The code already knew: `_forward_key`'s own comment documents `corH`, whose
tiles run (1,16)..(1,21) with the party entering at index 5, where the save
says `A` and the screen shows the entrance on the LEFT.

### A BACKWARD STEP IS INVISIBLE, which is what made it expensive

Per-tile `knowledge` and `light` are effectively monotonic. Over the corpus:
**108 growth events against 2 that ever shrank.** So a party walking the wrong
way writes nothing to the save and reads **exactly** like a party walking into
a wall.

That is why the two loops behaved as they did:

* `_walk_up_to` counted the non-advance as a stall and concluded *"something is
  in the way"* -- discarding the only direction signal it had;
* `_walk_toward` read the position once, walked up to `MAX_WALK_STEPS` blind,
  and learned only if it fell out of the corridor into the wrong area. That is
  a whole wasted traversal per mistake, **and no learning at all in a dead
  end**. On the Darkest Dungeon it is Fran's *"not useful to walk backwards
  because of enhanced stress"*.

### The fix: a stall is a DIRECTION hypothesis before it is an obstacle

Walking the wrong way and walking into something are indistinguishable from the
save, so the response has to cover both -- **flip first, blame the obstacle
second**. Once per corridor: a second stall after the flip really is scenery,
and oscillating on it would be the `rooH`/`corH` failure in a new costume.

`_flip_heading` is the single place that does it, and it updates
`heading_wrong` as well as `Heading`, because `_forward_key` re-derives the
save's measurement on every call and would otherwise overwrite the flip before
it was used. `_walk_toward` now reads the position **per step, and only to
STEER** -- the standing objection to reading it there is about *stopping*, and
still holds: the last step out of a corridor is the step that makes `walked` go
None, so stopping on a stalled position would end the walk nowhere near the
door.

It also plugs a gap on the other path: `Heading.observe` flipped the global
heading when a walk exited into the wrong area but left `heading_wrong` alone,
so the next `_forward_key` on that corridor put it straight back.

**Cost of a wrong guess: three steps instead of twelve-and-a-traversal, and it
now converges in a dead end.**

### A note on why this is not fixed geometrically

Tile order being monotonic in `mappos` means the geometry COULD name the
direction -- for horizontal corridors, if map-x-increasing is screen-right. For
the 23 vertical ones it could not without a second convention, and there is
exactly **one** ground-truth observation of what that convention is (`corH`,
where decreasing y was screen-right). One data point is not a rule, and a
guessed convention would be wrong silently on half the corridors it touched.
The stall-flip needs no convention and is driven by measurement, so it ships
instead. If a live run ever banks the pairing for a handful of vertical
corridors, the geometry becomes a free first-guess on top of it.


## RESOLVE LEVEL WAS A LEVEL SHORT, IN EVERY COMBAT (2026-09-02)

`engine/combat_engine.py` applied `max(0, level - 1) * 0.10` to
stun/blight/bleed/debuff/move. It should be `max(0, level) * 0.10`.

**The scale is the save's, 0-6.** `resolve_level_thresholds` ships seven
entries with the first at 0, `TIER_TO_HERO_LEVEL` is written on that scale, and
`RosterHero.resolve_level` carries it. So `base_stats` describes a **resolve-0**
hero — and the old comment's own justification, *"base_stats already reflect an
unleveled hero"*, argues for the corrected formula rather than for the
subtraction it sat above. Subtracting one gave **resolve 0 and resolve 1
identical resistances** and left every hero above 0 a level short.

Fran's worked example is what settled it, and both ends are shipped numbers:

    a level 0 leper has 60% but a level 6 leper has 60 base + 10*6% extra
    so 120%

The Leper ships `.stun 60% .move 60%`. The wiki agrees in one sentence —
*"Each resolve level increases hero chance to disarm traps and resistances to
stun, blight, bleed, move and debuff by 10%"* — which also confirms the five
resistances the engine already had were the RIGHT five. Only the offset was
wrong.

**120% is not a bug and nothing clamps it.** A resist is subtracted in
`roll < chance - resist`, so above 1.0 simply means the payload cannot land. A
clamp at 1.0 would silently discard the top four resolve levels for a hero like
the Leper, and `tests/env/test_hero_level.py` now pins the 1.20.

**THIS MOVED THE TRAINING DISTRIBUTION.** Every hero at resolve 1 or above is
more resistant than it was, so a comp score, a leaderboard or an eval from
before 2026-09-02 was measured on a different ruler. Checkpoints still load —
the observation is unchanged.

### It also closed the disagreement `bridge/traps.py` had just recorded

The trap layer had shipped `level * 0.10` a few hours earlier, with the engine's
subtraction documented as an open question in `docs/NEXT.md` and a test pinning
both sides so neither could drift shut by accident. That question is answered
and the item is gone; the test now asserts the two modules AGREE, by comparing
the engine's realised `stun_resist` bonus against the trap layer's at the same
resolve level. One rule living in two files is how a rule drifts.

## THE WIKI'S TRAP PAGE AGREES WITH THE FILES ON FOUR NUMBERS (2026-09-02)

Fran supplied the Trap page. Every number in it lands on something
`bridge/traps.py` reads out of shipped data, and none of the four was derived
from those files:

| claim | wiki | derived here |
|---|---|---|
| scouted disarm bonus | +40% | `trap_scout_disarm_bonus` **0.4** |
| Veteran / Champion / Darkest | +20 / +40 / +50% | `difficulty_trap_base[3,5,6]` = **0.2 / 0.4 / 0.5** |
| best trinket pair, Grave Robber | **+55%** | Ancestor's Map 0.25 + Raider's Talisman 0.30 = **0.55** |
| disarm stress heal | 8 | `Heal Stress TrapD` |

**The trinket row is the strongest.** Nobody derived "+55% for Grave Robbers
(Ancestor's Map + Raider Talisman)" from `base.buffs.json`, and the two buffs
sum to exactly that — putting a kitted Grave Robber on **105%**. Both halves
have to be right for it to come out. The difficulty row is the second: DD's
quest difficulties are Apprentice 1 / Veteran 3 / Champion 5 / Darkest 6, and
`difficulty_trap_base` indexes straight onto them, which is the check that the
index IS the quest difficulty rather than a bracket.

### And one real correction: a scouted trap is a different MECHANIC

    Trap Resist is the chance to dodge unscouted traps. If a trap has been
    scouted, the chance for a hero to disarm it is their Trap Resist + 40%.
    ... Disarming a trap renders it harmless and heals 8 stress for the hero.

The module had `scouted` as a modifier on one mechanic. It is two:

* **unscouted** — the trap fires on whoever walked into it, Trap Resist is a
  chance to DODGE, nothing heals stress, and **there is no hero to pick**;
* **scouted** — the trap is visible and offers a disarm at Trap Resist + 40%,
  and success heals 8 stress. This is the click, and the only case where
  "which hero" is a question.

`advise_trap` now refuses on an unscouted trap rather than naming somebody,
because naming a hero implies a choice the game is not offering.

**`disarm_chance` clamps to [0, 1]; the STAT does not**, and the wiki is
explicit that Disarm Chance can exceed 100%. That headroom is exactly what pays
for the Champion penalty, and it survives only because the difficulty is
subtracted *before* the clamp — a kitted, scouted Grave Robber is 1.45 less
0.40 = **1.05**, which clamps to a certainty rather than to 60%.


## WHO STEPS ON THE TRAP, AND WHO OPENS THE CURIO (2026-09-02)

Two of Fran's dungeon rules, and both turned out to be things the game states
rather than things to encode.

### The trap layer existed in the data all along

`bridge/curios.py` declared trap disarm uncoverable: *"Disarm chance is the
`resistance`/`trap` stat, built from class base plus quirks plus trinkets, and
neither `CombatantState` nor `live_state.py` carries it — inventing a ranking
would be a convention wearing game-data clothes."* That was right to refuse and
wrong about the data. `HeroStats.trap_resist` is parsed off every class's
`.info.darkest`, 33 buffs carry `stat_type "resistance" / stat_sub_type
"trap"`, and a live save carries the hero's quirks and trinkets.

**The derivation reproduces the folklore unaided**, which is the check that it
reads the right key: Grave Robber **50%**, Houndmaster / Highwayman / Bounty
Hunter **40%**, Flagellant **0%**. Nobody derived "the Grave Robber is the trap
hero" from `.info.darkest`, and it lands there.

**Fran's stress rule is in the files too.** He said *"trap defuse relieves
stress"*; every disarmable trap ships `success_effects: ["Heal Stress TrapD"]`
— poison_cloud, spikes, blade_wheel, lurker, blood_puddle and the Crimson
Court's slumbering_vampire. Failing costs `Stress 2` plus a DoT plus a fraction
of max HP. So the two regimes fall out of the data rather than being a
preference: above the threshold the disarm is a stress HEAL and goes to the
**most stressed**; below it the FAILURE is what to plan for and it goes to the
best odds, then the freshest. `spikes_ambush` ships no `success_effects` at all
and is excluded — it is the ambush variant, and offering a hero for it would be
advice about a choice DD never presents.

**The chance is a DECLARED READING with a cheap falsifier.** `rules.json` gives
`difficulty_trap_base = [0, 0, 0, 0.2, 0.2, 0.4, 0.5]` and
`trap_scout_disarm_bonus = 0.4`, and the composition is the `chance - resist`
shape every resisted roll here uses. DD draws the disarm percentage on the trap
tile's own hover text, so one live frame settles it; `DISARM_MEASURED` is False
and the advice line says "unverified formula" until then.

**The RANKING is trustworthy anyway, and a test proved the argument was
initially wrong.** The difficulty and scout terms are identical for every hero
on the tile, so they cancel out of a comparison — but only *before the clamp*.
Floored at 0, a Crusader's 0.10 and a Flagellant's 0.00 both read 0.00 at the
hardest difficulty and TIE, and the order between them falls to whatever the
sort does with equal keys. `disarm_margin` is the unclamped quantity everything
ranks on; `disarm_chance` is the clamped one that gets printed and tested
against the threshold. The invariance test now sweeps all seven difficulties
against both scout states and requires one ordering.

`DISARM_WORTH_TRYING = 0.50` is the one convention, and its source is Fran's
sentence rather than a file.

### Fran's correction: the class is not the only source

*"trinkets and resolve level also change the trap resistance value, so the
class isnt the only thing"* -- and he was pointing at two defects, one of them
in code that looked wired.

**`rule_type "always"` IS unconditional, and reading it as a gate is what made
the trinket path dead.** The first version skipped any buff carrying a truthy
`rule_type`, on the reasonable-sounding argument that a gate cannot be
evaluated without a board. But **22 of the 33 trap-resistance buffs carry
`always`** -- including Ancestor's Map (+25%), Raider's Talisman (+30%),
Survival Guide and Drifter's Buckle -- so four of the game's best trap trinkets
contributed exactly zero. The path existed, ran, and returned the base stat.

The gate is three-valued now instead of two: `always` and `""` are active,
`in_camp` / `firstroundonly` / `monsterType` are decidably **False** on a trap
tile (there is no camp, no combat round and no monster), `lightbelow` /
`lightabove` are decided against the torch the bridge already reads off the
raid save, and anything else is **UNKNOWN** -- skipped and *reported*, because
under-counting quietly is how a hero looks worse at traps than they are. Note
the direction differs from the engine on purpose: `_is_conditional_rule_active`
grants an unread rule, which is a defensible choice with a board in hand and
the wrong one without.

**Resolve level was missing outright.** +10% per level, on the SAVE's 0-6
scale (`resolve_level_thresholds` ships seven entries, the first being 0), so a
resolve-6 Crusader carrying Ancestor's Map reads **95%** where the class table
alone says 10%.

**And that exposed a disagreement with the engine that is NOT being closed
here.** `engine/combat_engine.py` applies `max(0, level - 1) * 0.10` on the
same 0-6 scale, which gives resolve 0 and resolve 1 identical resistances --
while its own comment justifies the formula by saying `base_stats` "already
reflect an unleveled hero", and an unleveled hero is resolve 0, not resolve 1.
The two readings differ by exactly one level.

The engine is deliberately left alone: it applies that bonus to
stun/blight/bleed/debuff/move for every hero in every combat, so changing it
moves the training distribution and is a decision rather than a tidy-up.
`tests/bridge/test_traps.py` pins the disagreement from both sides so it cannot be
closed by accident in either direction, and `docs/NEXT.md` carries the
falsifier: DD draws a hero's resistances on the character sheet, so one frame
of a resolve-0 and a resolve-1 hero of the same class settles it.

### The curio specialist is a shipped key, not a class name

*"for curio we should choose antiquarian if available"* — and
`heroes/antiquarian/antiquarian.info.darkest` carries `extra_curio_loot: .code
"ANTIQ" .count 1`, beside an `extra_battle_loot` with the same code. So
`extra_curio_loot_classes` reads the key across every hero file, base and DLC,
and a modded class shipping it is handled with no entry anywhere — the same
argument `unrestricted_heroes` and `hero_value.skill_slots` rest on.

**`Loot` had NO hero rule at all**, which is why this was worth doing rather
than a nicety: it is 41 of the curio library's outcomes and 27 of its item
interactions, and every one of them fell through `_pick_hero` to "no opinion".
The one hero the game gives a documented curio bonus to was never named.

The existing rules still win where they apply — a Purge still goes to the worst
quirks, a stress-relief result still goes to the most stressed. Note for anyone
testing this through `advise` rather than `_pick_hero`: `advise` picks the
curio's **best-valued outcome** first, so `holy_fountain` relieves stress on one
roll and still routes to the Antiquarian, because its best outcome is Loot at
50%. That is the outcome ranking, not the hero rule.


## THE RETREAT VALUATION WAS 31x LOW (2026-09-02)

Fran, after testing the act model in a dungeon:

    Cost evaluation/retreat evaluation is wrong, each hero starts with 4 skills
    (outside of 7 skill heroes) so each one that has other skills unlocked is
    +1000 per other skill unlocked +X per level of each skill +Y per level of
    armor +Z per level of weapon, +N per positive quirk locked, +All the value
    of the inventory

He is right, and the size of it is the point: `party_investment` priced his
Week 22 roster of thirty heroes at **750 gold**. It is **23,750**.

**One line did most of it.** `hero_investment`'s `skill_rank` defaults to the
weapon rank, so a hero with rank-0 gear got `skill_ranks={s: 0}` and every
skill they owned summed to nothing — a Crusader with three bought 1000-gold
unlocks priced at **zero**. Being un-upgraded in gear made their skills free.

Three defects, all leaning the same way — they priced what is EQUIPPED rather
than what was BOUGHT:

* **It charged for the four skills the recruit arrives with.** Rank 1 of a
  skill tree is 1000 gold and a fresh hero already owns four trees at rank 1.
  Whenever gear HAD been bought, that inflated the hero by up to 4000 — so the
  same function was wrong in both directions depending on gear rank.
* **It read `selected_skills`, the four EQUIPPED.** A hero who unlocked six and
  equipped four was priced as though the other two were never bought. That is
  exactly the "+1000 per other skill unlocked" term, and it is the half that
  grows across a campaign.
* **It assumed every skill sat at the weapon's rank.** A stated convention,
  made when nothing could read the real ranks. `data/hero_skills.py` reads them
  per skill now, so the convention is unnecessary and wrong for any hero
  upgraded unevenly.

### `data/hero_value.py`, and the three things it derives rather than assumes

* **The free slot count is DERIVED.** `skill_selection:
  .number_of_selected_combat_skills_max` is 4 on eighteen classes and 7 on
  exactly the two carrying `can_select_combat_skills false`. Reproducing that
  split from a *different key in the same file* is the check that it reads the
  right thing — and it is what prices a modded stance class correctly with no
  entry anywhere, the same argument `unrestricted_heroes(store)` rests on.
* **The unlock price is read from the tree**, not typed in. 1000 in all twenty
  shipped trees and all four campaign modes.
* **Free to OWN is not free to UPGRADE.** The 250/750/1250/2500 ladder above
  the unlock is charged on every skill a hero has, including the four that
  came free.

### `is_locked` is a real bool, and the POSITIVE filter is load-bearing

`persist.roster.json` stores `quirks.<id>.is_locked` as a **1-byte DSON bool**,
unlike `persist.upgrades.json`'s `is_purchased`, which decodes to nothing on
every one of its 228 entries. Over both live campaigns: 25 `False`, **1
`True`**, 115 absent.

That single `True` is why the side filter exists rather than being tidiness: it
is `torn_rotator_cuff` on a Vestal — a **negative** quirk. The game locks
diseases and permanent negatives itself, and nobody paid 7500 gold for one.
Counting every locked quirk would price a diseased hero as carrying a bought
positive. `positive_locked` requires `store.quirks[id].is_positive`, and an
unknown id is excluded rather than assumed positive.

The lock ladder is shipped: **7500 / 6750 / 6000 / 5250 / 4500 / 3750** by
Sanitarium tier. Tier 0 is the default because it is the DEAREST rate, which
makes the party look expensive and therefore argues for pulling out — the same
conservative direction `gold_per_stress` takes.

### Trinkets are deliberately NOT priced

DD does not sell them and `base.entries.trinkets.json` carries no price field,
so `InventoryValue` counts them and prices only what the game itself prices:
carried gold, and provisions at the provisioner's own rate. Heirlooms are
counted too — the exchange trades them against each other and never against
gold, so they have no gold rate to read. Inventing one would put a convention
inside a decision that reads as game data.

### The fallback is kept, and it is a floor

`party_investment(heroes, game_dir)` with no `profile` still uses the old
approximation, because a replayed capture has no save beside it and a rough
number beats refusing to price a retreat. It is documented as a floor and
never the figure to quote.

**Fran's larger point is still open**: *"later in the game, the value of each
roster that you have invested in will increase to even have more value than the
full money of the dungeon"*. This makes the number right at a moment; it does
not yet make it a curve, and the retreat decision still compares one party
against one quest reward rather than against what the roster will be worth.


## THE EMBARK CHAIN WAS FLOWN, AND THE FRAME FOUND TWO REAL BUGS (2026-09-02)

Fran left the game on the embark page and said to test autonomously. The chain
ran end to end -- quest selected, comp seated in its own formation, 3550-gold
basket bought, raid entered with **no underprepared dialog**, which is the tell
that the party and the basket are both right. Two defects fell out of flying
it, and both were invisible to the suite and to every dry run.

### THE ROSTER LIST ARRIVES SCROLLED, AND THE SAVE DOES NOT SAY SO

`town_roster_list_layout.element_max` is 8 and the campaign holds 12, and the
list was sitting at **offset 4** before anything touched it. Every caller was
passing a SAVE index into `roster_portrait`, so `seat_hero(0)` would have
grabbed `town.heroes[4]`.

**It would have reported OK.** The party bar changes when the wrong hero lands
in it exactly as it does for the right one, so the acknowledgement channel
could not have caught this -- which is what makes it worse than a misclick.

It had been harmless by accident: `week_skip` and `recruit_all` were written
when the roster held eight or fewer, where no scroll is possible.

**Nothing in the save carries the offset.** `persist.roster.json` has 33 fields
per hero and not one of them is a name, a sort key or a scroll position. What
it does carry is the class list in list order, and the game draws its own
portrait art -- so the offset is recoverable from PIXELS. `town._roster_offset`
matches the eight drawn portraits against the class sequence at every candidate
offset and takes the best alignment. Eight rows over-determine one integer,
which is what makes it robust when one portrait matches poorly under the game's
colour grading, and it **refuses** below a 2.0 margin because a wrong offset
drags a different hero rather than missing.

Measured walking the live list 4 -> 3 -> 2 -> 1 -> 0, one click each: the right
offset won by **5.6 to 6.9** mean levels every time.

**So `ROSTER_SCROLL_MEASURED` is now True, and the reason it was False
inverted.** It was False because "a mis-scrolled list silently re-indexes every
row" -- and the list was already mis-scrolled, so refusing to scroll was a way
of not noticing rather than a way of being safe. Once the offset is measurable
the re-indexing is not silent, and scrolling is confirmed the way a purchase
is.

**And the chevrons ARE shipped geometry, once anchored to the right thing.**
Composing `roster_list_pos + top/bottom_frame_offset + scroll_*_button_offset`
puts them at design y **-88 and -22** -- above the top of the screen. They are
anchored to the ROW BAND (`element_pos.y` 132 to `+ element_spacing.y *
element_max` 908), each **12 design px outside its own edge**, 12 being
`scroll_down_button_offset`'s own magnitude:

| chevron | derived | hue-measured live | residual |
|---|---|---|---|
| up | 120.0 | **119.8** | -0.20 |
| down | 920.0 | **919.7** | -0.30 |

One shipped constant applied to both edges, and the two share an x to the
pixel, which is the check that they are one widget mirrored. Only the x needs a
measured residual (+10.3).

### A PARKED CURSOR DRAWS A TOOLTIP, AND A TOOLTIP BREAKS `recognise`

The basket bought perfectly -- HUD gold **6,670 -> 3,120**, exactly 3,550, every
stock count down by exactly what was bought -- and then `walk` sat on "an
unrecognised screen for 16 s" and gave up **on the provision screen with the
Embark button lit**.

Nothing was wrong with the buying or the walking. The cursor was still on the
last torch cell it clicked, DD drew that item's tooltip, and the tooltip covers
`screen.SIG_BOX` (x 0.34-0.65, y 0.28-0.63) -- the middle of the frame, which
is the only part the recogniser looks at. `_look` returned `''`.

`_park_cursor` moves the mouse to the far-left edge after every town send and
every drag. Re-measured immediately: `''` -> `'provision'`, then Embark ->
`dungeon_intro` -> in the raid. **The fix is one mouse move and it converted a
dead loop into a working embark.**

This is a general hazard rather than a provisioner one: any click leaves the
cursor on the widget it clicked, and DD tooltips are large and centre-screen.

### THE PROVISIONER'S SAVE DOES NOT MOVE, AND THE SHELF DOES

All eight purchases reported `UNCONFIRMED (the wallet did not move)` while the
HUD showed the gold gone to the last coin. That is the documented town save lag
-- and reporting eight working purchases as failures is worse than useless,
because the run below treats them as not done.

The channel that does move is the **shop cell**, which redraws its remaining
stock per item. That is both a channel that exists and a stricter one than the
wallet: it is per item rather than "some gold left". Same pixel-difference test
`_bar_moved` runs on the party bar. Verified both ways -- a purchase reads OK,
and two idle re-grabs of four cells read False, so it is not a rubber stamp.

### What the frame confirmed for free

* the **crypts quest token** lands dead on the Ruins emblem, live, independently
  of the `tools/find_button.py` measurement it was derived against;
* all eight **roster rows** and all four **party ranks by combat rank**, with
  rank 1 rightmost;
* every one of the **nine provisioner cells**, with the drawn prices and stock
  counts matching `data/provisions.py`'s parsed values item for item;
* the party that actually embarked is `Iron Shackles: Money` in comp order --
  crusader#1, abomination#31, antiquarian#20, plague_doctor#7 at ranks 1-4.

**A tenth shop cell exists that is not in the store list**: a Color of Madness
shard item, drawn at index 9 after torch at 8. It is appended, so the mapping is
undisturbed -- but an item inserted EARLIER would shift every index, and the
mapping is derived from `default_store_inventory_item_lists` rather than
measured, so that is the thing to re-check if a DLC ever changes the shelf.


## THE EMBARK CHAIN IS DECIDED OFFLINE (2026-09-02)

Fran's next phase, in his order: *"EMBARK, scout missions and place comp, go
back to hamlet into guild/blacksmith, upgrade comp, set attacks for the comp
and go back to embark and start the mission"*, with three decisions attached —
only ruins/warrens/weald/cove, a **random** curated comp among those that work
rather than the best one, and provisions reserved first at the **recommended**
end.

`bridge/embarkplan.py` answers all of it with **no policy, no checkpoint and
no screen**. That is deliberate: `bridge/embark.py` still exists and still
ranks parties by running the model, but the ask here was *"rather than just
choose the best option I would like just an option that works"*, so the score
is affordability and the choice among the affordable is `rng.choice`.

Four findings made it cheap, and each was measured before a line was written.

### 1. The estate map's quest tokens are SHIPPED GEOMETRY

`REGION_SCENE_POINTS` held one hand-measured region (the Darkest Dungeon, for
the week skip) and every other quest would have needed its own measurement.
None of them does. `campaign/town/quest_select/quest_select.layout.darkest`
gives each dungeon a `quest_map_pos`, and `town_quest_select_dungeon_layout`
gives `quest_button_start_offset 160 92`. Matched against a live estate map
with `tools/find_button.py` on the game's own quest-type icons, at 1280x720:

| dungeon | `quest_map_pos` | derived + (8, 92) | measured live |
|---|---|---|---|
| crypts | 940 220 | (948, 312) | **(948.1, 312.0)** |
| darkestdungeon | 1260 100 | (1268, 192) | **(1267.6, 192.0)** |
| courtyard | 570 330 | (578, 422) | **(578.3, 422.3)** |

Three dungeons, **two different layout files** (the courtyard is in the Crimson
Court's own `crimson_court.quest_select.layout.darkest`, under a different
filename), both axes inside 0.5 design px. The **y is the file's own 92
verbatim**; only the x carries a measured residual, and it is **8** rather than
the file's 160 — the same derived-plus-one-constant shape as
`UPGRADE_NODE_OFFSET` and `CLOSE_GLYPH_CENTRE`.

**A second quest in one region still refuses.** `quest_button_spacing 76 80`
has never been observed, because every live map so far has held at most one
quest per dungeon, and two readings fit the single measured point equally well
(the row starts there and runs right, or it is centred on it). For one quest
those are the same pixel; for two they are not, and a wrong point selects a
*different quest*. `QUEST_ROW_MEASURED` is False and `quest_button(d, 1)`
returns None.

### 2. A hero's unlocked skills are in the save, so affordability needs no screen

`persist.upgrades.json`'s `instance_number` **is the hero's guid**. Three
independent agreements over the live twelve-hero roster say so:

* every hash that inverts for an instance names a tree of **that hero's own
  class** — never another's;
* the ten restricted classes carry **exactly four** combat trees at rank 1,
  which is exactly `selected_combat_skills`;
* the Abomination and the Duelist carry **all seven**, which is
  `skill_selection: .can_select_combat_skills false` — `UNRESTRICTED_HEROES`
  falling out of the save with nothing in `data/hero_skills.py` testing for
  identity.

**The leftover hashes are the camp trees, and the count is the hero's own.**
The first version of this claim was "exactly three", from twelve heroes that
all had three camping skills. `tests/data/test_hero_skills.py` ran against the other
live profile and `flagellant#60` falsified it in one line: two camping skills,
two unnamed hashes. The rule is `len(unaccounted) ==
len(hero.camping_skills)`, which is a stronger claim than the constant it
replaced and is what the test now pins.

**`upgrades/heroes/` holds only the fifteen vanilla classes.** Musketeer,
Flagellant, Shieldbreaker, Duelist and Runaway ship their own copy under
`dlc/**/upgrades/heroes/`, so `load_hero_costs` returned `None` for all five
and a Duelist read as a hero with no skills, no ranks and nothing to buy —
silently, because `unlocked` returns `{}` for a class it cannot price.
`hero_upgrades_path` globs the DLC roots, **excluding `modes/`**, which leaves
exactly one file per class, twenty of twenty. The three
`modes/{radiant,bloodmoon,new_game_plus}` variants differ only in weapon and
armour prices (Duelist weapon rank 2 is 1500 / 1750 / 2000); **every skill rank
is 1000 / 250 / 750 / 1250 / 2500 in all four**, so the campaign mode cannot
change what an unlock costs.

### 3. A skill unlock is 1000 gold and needs nothing else; gear is not

`<class>.<skill>` code `0` has **no prerequisites and
`prerequisite_resolve_level: 0`** in all twenty trees. `<class>.weapon` code
`0` is 750 gold but requires `blacksmith.weapon a` — **8 deed + 8 crest against
a wallet holding 0 deed** — and resolve 1, which two of the twelve live heroes
have. Hence Fran's "skills only for now", and hence `_visit_guild` buying while
the blacksmith only reports.

### 4. The provisioner's item order is shipped, not measured

`provision.json`'s `default_store_inventory_item_lists[length]` lists the stock
in the order the panel draws it: food, shovel, antivenom, bandage,
medicinal_herbs, skeleton_key, holy_water, laudanum, torch. **Nine items into a
7-column grid puts torch at index 8** — exactly where
`bridge/layout.py::PROVISION_TORCH` was independently hand-clicked, with food
at 0 where `PROVISION_FOOD` is. Two constants a human measured, reproduced by a
file nobody derived them from, which is the check that the reading is right.
`townlayout.shop_item` composes `shop_slot(index) + ICON_CENTRE_OFFSET
(40, 72)` and `tests/bridge/test_townlayout.py` requires both to land on the same cell.

`confirm_datas[length].minimum_food` is **0 / 8 / 8 / 12 / 12 / 12** — the
game's own underprepared-dialog floor. The bridge's flat `FOOD_TO_BUY = 8` was
right for a short quest and four short for a medium one; `basket` now reads it.

### The provision basket is PLAYBOOK 1.5, and it is the only convention here

`data/provisions.py` is two halves that must not be confused. `GUIDE` is Fran's
own table per zone and per band, a **declared convention with a named source** —
the same status `BASE_PROVISION_AMOUNTS` documents itself as, except that this
one has an author. Prices, stock, draw order and the food floor are all parsed.
Ruins/short at the recommended end prices at **3550 gold**, which is the number
the skill budget is `gold - 3550`.

`CUT_ORDER` is the other policy: shovels go first and **food and torches go
last**, because the dark is a damage modifier on every fight and the food floor
is a dialog.

### What the plan produces on the live campaign

`python bridge/embarkplan.py --seed 0`, against 6670 gold and a roster of 12:

```
quest   crypts explore (difficulty 1, length 1)  reward 3000g
comp    Iron Shackles: Money
gold    6670  -> 120 (provisions 3550, unlocks 3000)
  rank 1  crusader#1        buy battle_heal, inspiring_cry (2000g)
  rank 2  abomination#31
  rank 3  antiquarian#20    buy invigorating_vapours (1000g)
  rank 4  plague_doctor#7
note    no curated comp fits the 3120g skill budget (cheapest of 10 is 4000g)
note    keeping their own skills: plague_doctor
```

**The fallback path is the likely one, not an edge case**, and that is what
makes "an option that works" honest: ten curated comps are fieldable from
twelve heroes and the cheapest needs 4000 gold of unlocks against a 3120
budget. `_trim_to_budget` drops unlocks **from the back rank forward**, so the
front rank — which takes the hits — is equipped first and the rest keep the
four skills they already have.

Four rules the module rests on:

* **`fill_cheapest`, not `embark.fill_shape`.** That one picks lowest-stress
  first within a class, which is right when a rollout is about to rank the
  parties. Here the question is whether the comp is affordable at all, and the
  live roster's two Antiquarians differ in one skill — choosing by stress
  reports a comp unaffordable that is affordable with the other one.
* **Only `curated_comps/`.** `CompLoadoutEntry.source` is new and carries the
  folder a comp was loaded from. `composed_comps/` is training coverage that
  nobody has played; sending a live party on one is exactly the claim the
  two-folder split exists to keep separable.
* **The formation is the comp's own hero order.** `CombatEngine.reset` assigns
  `position = index`, so index 0 is rank 1, and `party_slot_for_rank` puts rank
  1 in the **rightmost** slot. Seating a rank-ordered party into slots 0..3
  embarks it reversed.
* **A comp declaring fewer than four heroes is never offered.** `The Old Road`
  is the tutorial pair, is fieldable from this roster, and costs 0 — so it led
  the `--all` listing until the size filter moved into `fieldable_comps`, the
  one function both the plan and the listing now call. That duplication is how
  `pick_representative_comps` and `build_comp_pool` drifted.

### `is_generated` refused the only quest on the map

`bridge/embark.py` filtered `q.is_generated and q.simulable` and printed "no
simulable generated quest on the map" on a map holding one. The live Ruins
quest is `plot_tutorial_crypts` — a plot quest, and a perfectly ordinary Ruins
expedition with a real encounter table. What genuinely has none is the
courtyard and the Darkest Dungeon, which is what `simulable` already excludes.
Both modules now filter on `simulable` alone.

### What is built but NOT flown, and why each one refuses

Layer 3's rule is that an unmeasured coordinate refuses rather than guesses,
and three of these are one live visit from being un-gated:

* **`BUILDING_SCENE_POINTS["guild"]` does not exist.** A building is entered by
  clicking it in the Hamlet scene; the scene x is derivable from
  `town.layout.darkest` and the **y is not** (the scene is a perspective
  projection with a per-building z). So the guild step refuses before it opens
  anything.
* **`GUILD_MEASURED = False`.** The guild's rows compose from
  `guild_layout.skill_pos 44 -4` / `skill_spacing 0 91` and
  `guild_upgrade_tree_layout.requirement_spacing 75 0`, the same shape
  `upgrade_node` uses. The stage coach is the reason to distrust that: its
  derived nodes landed in the panel's left margin until a live frame moved them
  by (133.5, 35.3), and **no tree was drawn at all until the building crest was
  clicked**. `_visit_guild` opens the panel, banks the frame, and stops.
* **Which element of a guild skill row is the SELECTION toggle** — as opposed
  to the first purchase — is unresolved, and is precisely what that banked
  frame has to settle. `plan_select_skill` composes the point and refuses.
* **`ROSTER_SCROLL_MEASURED = False`, and this one has a real cost.** The list
  shows 8 of 12, so a comp naming a hero on row 8+ cannot be seated. Refusing
  anyway is right because a mis-scrolled list silently **re-indexes every row**
  — the drag still lands, on somebody else. Composing the arrows also caught a
  reading error worth keeping: anchoring both frames to `roster_list_pos` alone
  puts them at design y **-88 and -22**, above the top of the screen. They are
  anchored to the row band, which puts them at 44 and 886, bracketing rows that
  run 180 to 859.


## THE POST-TUTORIAL PHASE RAN END TO END (2026-09-01)

Fran deleted the save and asked for the whole chain: boot -> tutorial -> the
Hamlet's first visit -> two week skips. It finished, with a **roster of 12** --
his own arithmetic, 3 tutorial-era heroes + the Vestal + 4 + 4 -- and the
Vestal home at 100 stress, exactly the hero he said to dismiss afterwards.

Five bugs fell out of flying it, and every one was invisible to the suite.

### The stage coach keeps TWO stores and only one is drawn

Two hires came back `UNCONFIRMED (the roster did not grow)` while the panel
showed one recruit and the save listed five:

```
row=0 slot= 8 hero_recruit         vestal
row=0 slot= 9 shard_hero_recruit   runaway
row=1 slot=10 shard_hero_recruit   bounty_hunter
row=2 slot=11 shard_hero_recruit   hellion
row=3 slot=12 shard_hero_recruit   abomination
```

`read_recruits` pools `hero_recruit` with Color of Madness's
`shard_hero_recruit`, and `Hire.index` was the pooled position -- so the drags
started at rows 3 and 4 of a column that draws one. The shard store is bought
with **shards**, a currency nothing here spends and a fresh campaign has none
of, so it is not a row a drag can ever reach.

`Recruit.row` is the fix: the position WITHIN its own listing, numbered after
the sort because the entries come out of a dict keyed by the save's slot.
`choose_recruits` draws only from `GOLD_RECRUIT_LISTING`. The distinction
`slot` / `row` / pooled-index now has one name each.

### `_party_bar` cropped a frame with DESKTOP coordinates

The party-drag acknowledgement channel was blind: `_party_bar` grabbed a
1280x720 frame and then sliced it at `resolved.at(...)`, which answers in
screen pixels (~1300, ~790 with the window where it sat). The slice was empty,
the crop returned `None`, `_bar_moved` failed safe to False, and a live run
reported **five drags UNCONFIRMED while the frame showed the hero seated**.
`_img_pt` is the frame-space converter that already existed for the exchange
probe; the same confusion, in a second place.

### The party bar is inert until a quest is selected

Fran's procedure reads embark -> clear -> seat -> pick the quest, which is
right at the keyboard because the previous week's quest is still lit when you
arrive. From a fresh estate map nothing is selected and **every drag into the
party bar is silently ignored** -- five sent, bar unchanged, and the identical
drag succeeded the moment a quest was lit. `week_skip` picks the quest first.
The game's ordering, not his.

### An OCCUPIED roster row is a valid drop target

`_park_row` refused whenever the roster filled all 8 visible rows, on the
theory that dropping onto an occupant is a reorder rather than an unseat, and
`_drop_row`'s ancestor gave up with "no roster row 8". A roster of 8 is the
ordinary state after one week skip, so between them they blocked the clear
AND the second batch of hires.

Measured instead of argued: with 8 heroes and the Vestal seated at rank 1,
dragging her portrait onto row 7 emptied the party bar and left
`persist.roster.json` **in exactly the same order**. Both now clamp to the last
visible row.

### A screen can have more than one LOOK

Every week skip afflicts its hero, and the raid-progression screen then draws a
pulsing glow over that hero's row. On those frames it sits **0.28 from its own
reference and 0.33 from `raid_progression`** -- inside `THRESHOLD` but well
inside `MARGIN`, so `recognise` correctly answered "I do not know" and the walk
home stalled for 16 s, twice.

`captures/screens/<name>@<variant>.png` banks a second look at one screen.
Two rules make it safe rather than a hole in the recogniser: `rank` returns the
BASE name, and the runner-up it measures the margin against is the nearest
reference **of a different screen** -- two looks at one screen are agreement,
not ambiguity, and without that rule banking a variant would make recognition
strictly worse.

### And `rostersize.a` turned out to be load-bearing

It was off the default upgrade path until the day before, on the reasonable
argument that a bigger roster is worth nothing while the roster is nearly
empty. The week skip falsifies it from shipped data:
`stage_coach.building.json` gives `roster_size_upgrades` **9 -> a:12**, and
2 tutorial heroes + 2 skips x 4 hires is **ten**. Without it the last three
hires of this run had nowhere to go.

## THE STAGE COACH WORKS, end to end, live (2026-09-01)

Fran sat the game in the Hamlet and said to try it. Both halves of the coach
landed on the first attempt.

### It bought an upgrade

`stage_coach.numrecruits.a`, priced `3 deed + 4 crest` in
`upgrades/building/stage_coach.upgrades.json`. Read off the HUD before and
after:

```
before   bust 10   portrait 10   deed 12   crest 24
after    bust 10   portrait 10   deed  9   crest 20
```

**Exactly -3 deed and -4 crest, with bust and portrait untouched.** The
heirloom cost reader, the tree ordering, the crest that reveals the trees and
the node geometry were built from four different sources and they agree with
what the game charged, to the unit.

### It hired a recruit

`recruit_slot(0)` dragged to `roster_slot(2)`: **Boteler, Seeker Plague
Doctor** joined the roster, which went 2/12 to 3/12. That is the drag
primitive, `RECRUIT_PORTRAIT_OFFSET` (measured off the panel frame, +39/+39
design because `store_item_pos` anchors the row and a drag needs the portrait),
and `roster_slot`'s `element_max` bound all working together.

### The acknowledgement had to come from the HUD, not the save

Worth recording because it inverts the bridge's usual rule. **The live campaign
had not been written to disk** -- the screen showed deed 12 / crest 24 and no
profile on disk matched -- so `visit_stage_coach`'s save check would have
reported UNCONFIRMED whatever happened, and did on an earlier attempt for
exactly this reason.

The wallet and the roster count are both DRAWN, so a pixel comparison of the
currency strip and the "N/12" badge settled it in one step. Save-is-truth
remains right for a raid, where the save is written per turn; **in town the
save can lag a whole session**, and the HUD is the more immediate channel. A
future `visit_stage_coach` should read both -- the save when it moves, the HUD
when it does not.

Also found while looking for the live profile: **`read_town_state` given a
profile directory with missing files silently falls back to another one.**
`profile_9` has no `persist.estate.json` and still returned a full wallet. That
is a footgun for anything that trusts the profile it passed in.

## The crest opens the upgrade trees, and the derivation was right (2026-09-01)

Fran sat the game in the Hamlet and said to test. Two gates lift, one stays.

### The upgrade trees: gate LIFTED

`plan_buy_upgrade` had been refusing on `UPGRADES_MEASURED` because no tree is
drawn on a freshly opened stage coach panel. **The building CREST opens them**
-- the emblem beside the panel title, which `building_base_layout.
upgrade_base_pos` anchors to within 4 design px in y. Clicking it revealed all
three trees; `captures/stage_coach_upgrades.png` is the frame.

What that frame confirms, before any correction is applied:

| | measured | shipped |
|---|---|---|
| node pitch | 47.7 px of 1280 = **71.6 design** | `requirement_spacing` **70** |
| tree pitch | 106.5 px of 1280 = **159.8 design** | `upgrade_trees_spacing` **160** |
| rank counts | **5, 5, 3** | numrecruits 5, rostersize 5, upgraded_recruits 3 |
| first price | **3 deed + 4 crest** | `numrecruits.a` = deed 3 + crest 4 |
| tree order | Stagecoach Network, Hero Barracks, Experienced Recruits | file order |

Both pitches, all three rank counts, the tree order and the price of the first
node are the shipped values. **The derivation was right about everything except
where the row starts** -- the residual is a single origin offset,
`UPGRADE_NODE_OFFSET` (133.5, 35.3) design, measured off that frame. With it,
all 13 nodes land within **0.3 px in x and 1.0 px in y** of their detected box
centres, and the overlay puts every marker dead centre.

That price agreement is the part worth keeping: the heirloom reader, the tree
order and the panel geometry were built from three different files and they
now agree with what the game draws.

### The heirloom exchange: gate STAYS DOWN

The toggle works -- `HEIRLOOM_EXCHANGE_BUTTON` opens "Trade Heirlooms", banked
as `captures/heirloom_exchange_panel.png` -- and `exchange_confirm(2)` lands on
the real confirm button, `exchange_amount(0, down)` on the real down arrow. The
to-column is ~11 px low with a 47 design pitch against the shipped 44.

**It stays gated on the from-selector**, which draws two up arrows and one down
arrow over a single slot rather than the horizontal row of four that
`choice_spacing 44 0` implies. Picking the wrong from-slot does not fail
safely: it spends the wrong heirloom, and the wallet check can only say so
afterwards. Every other town action either reveals a widget or is acknowledged
before it costs anything.

**And screen recognition cannot confirm this panel at all** -- `SIG_BOX` is the
centre block and the panel is bottom left, so `recognise` still returns
`hamlet` with it open. The `_panel_is_open` trick that works for buildings does
not transfer; confirming the exchange needs a pixel test inside the panel's own
rectangle.

## The curio interaction distance, MEASURED against Fran's own hands (2026-09-01)

He parked the party at the tutorial tent himself -- *"on the tutorial i did
stop in front of the curio, so you can check it out the optimal distance to
interact"* -- which turns a guessing game into a measurement.
`captures/curio_correct_distance.png` is that frame.

The marker is the front hero's gold selection platform, detected by hue in
both frames rather than by eye:

```
Fran (interacts)        platform x 541-617   centre 579
bridge (fails)          platform x 411-499   centre 455
                                             -------------
                                             124 px short
```

**And the comparison is valid**, which is the part that had to be checked
before believing it: cross-correlating the scene band of the two frames puts
the backgrounds within **2 px** of each other, so the camera has not moved and
the party positions compare directly.

**No new change follows from it, and that is the useful conclusion.**
`CURIO_NUDGE_HOLD` was already cut from `WALK_HOLD` (0.9 s) to 0.25 s with the
tries raised to 10, on the separate argument that one walk press steps over
the interaction window. At roughly 28 px a nudge, 124 px is **~4.5 nudges** --
comfortably inside the ten. The measurement therefore CONFIRMS the budget
rather than demanding another constant, and stacking a 124 px initial
correction on top of it would overshoot.

The number is worth keeping anyway: it is the first time "how far short is the
bridge" has been a quantity rather than an impression, and any future change
to the approach can be checked against it in one line.

## RETRACTED: the party was at the ENTRANCE, not the exit (2026-09-01)

The stall frame is the whole finding. `iter_20260901_003327_002_stall.png`
shows Reynauld and Dismas pressed against a **closed barred gate** at the end
of the corridor, 33/33 HP, nothing attacking them, having gone nowhere for
180 s while the same map click was retried three times.

Fran, watching it: *"it keeps reloading the file on the hallway"* and *"you
can see its the hallway because there is the blue arrow on the map towards the
second room (end of hallway)"*.

**The game already documents the answer, in a string this file quotes
elsewhere.** `tutorial_popup_using_doors_description`: *"To enter a door,
[CLICK] or press [W] when standing in front of it"* -- the same `W` that opens
a curio, and `_interact_curio`'s docstring cites it. A party already standing
at the door needs no coordinates.

`_try_the_door` was written to press `W` when the map click fails, and
**reverted within the hour, unflown**, because Fran named the geometry that
makes it harmful:

> *"they werent at the exit they were at the entrance, this is because the
> entrances of a hallway always are at the left side and the exit at the right
> side, you always advance towards the right."*

The party in that frame is at the far LEFT. The gate beside them is the
entrance they ARRIVED through, so `W` there walks them **backwards** into the
previous area -- and `_interact_curio`'s docstring already records exactly
that from a live run: *"the party enters a corridor standing at the door it
just came through, so the one thing in front of it is the room it just
left... it exited properly but immediately used W instead of D."*

**The lesson is not that `W` is wrong.** It opens doors and the game says so.
It is that **a door has a SIDE**, and nothing in that code path knows which
side the party is on. Direction is what it needs, and `Heading` is where that
would come from.

Three theories about this one stall are now retracted -- the exit room's
styling, the party token merging cells, and the door. The first two came from
reading the map panel's own output instead of a picture of the party. The
third came from reading the picture without knowing which way the party
faces. **A frame answers "where are they"; it does not answer "which way is
forward", and this dungeon layer turns entirely on the second.**

## RETRACTED: the map panel's box detection was not the bug (2026-09-01)

Two iterations in a row died in `rooA` of the tutorial, which is the one place
every run has to leave through, so this currently blocks the Hamlet entirely.
Both refused correctly rather than clicking blind:

```
[route] NOT clicking the map: rooB is not drawn on the map
        (24.0 px/unit, 4/5 boxes explained, worst 6.0 px; predicted 1014,624)
```

`captures/_map_panel.png`, brightened and zoomed, shows **six** boxes: `rooA`
green with the party's torch token on its right edge, four grey corridor
cells, and `rooB` drawn **BLUE with a fast-forward exit icon** -- a completely
different style from every other box on the panel.

**The transform is not the problem.** Measured off that frame, `rooB` centres
on **(943, 625)** and the detector predicted **(1014, 624)**: the y is exact to
a pixel and only the x is out, by 71 px. A fit that had genuinely gone wrong
would miss in both axes. What it looks like instead is one box never entering
the fit -- "4/5 explained" -- so the scale along x is derived from a shorter
run of boxes and `rooB` is extrapolated past its real position.

**Both hypotheses in this entry were WRONG, and the way they were wrong is
the point.**

The blue-styling guess died in two minutes: run offline over the banked frame,
`find_boxes` locates the exit room at (942.5, 618.5), within a pixel of where
it is drawn. The second guess -- that the party's orange token merges `rooA`
with the corridor cell beside it -- survived a box count and a width
measurement, was implemented, and **made the route click the wrong box**.
Across four live runs at one window origin:

```
6/6 boxes explained -> clicked client (942, 618) = rooB    correct
6/6 boxes explained -> clicked client (942, 618) = rooB    correct
5/6 boxes explained -> clicked client (894, 624) = a cell  WRONG   <- with the mask
```

The detector was **already explaining every box and aiming correctly**. The
mask split `rooA` into a 66 px room plus a phantom 23 px cell, one box then
matched no tile, and the fit shifted by a cell. Reverted; the helper is kept
unused with the evidence, and `tests/bridge/test_mappanel.py` pins that it is NOT
wired in.

**The reasoning error is worth more than the code.** `captures/_map_panel.png`
is banked **only on failure**, so it is a biased sample of size one -- and "90
px is too wide for `rooA`" came from eyeballing a brightened crop rather than
from any frame where the route had worked. A 90 px `rooA` is simply what that
room measures. Three position theories had already been falsified in this
module by reasoning from the save instead of the screen; this is the fourth,
and it was falsified by reasoning from ONE screen instead of several.

**What is actually open**: a map click sometimes does not register even when
aimed correctly -- twice out of eight tonight. That is a separate problem from
box detection and must not be "fixed" in the detector again.

**It is intermittent, which is consistent.** Map clicks across the night ran
**6 of 8 confirmed**; the two failures are these. A style the detector handles
only sometimes fits that better than a broken transform would.

## The tutorial tent: diagnosed, not a key problem (2026-09-01)

Fran's tutorial note was *"It doesnt interact with the tent curio"*. Captured
live, with frames at both ends:

```
[act] [curio] step 1..4 up to travellers_tent_tutorial on tile 4
[act] [curio] at tile 4, on travellers_tent_tutorial (tile 4)
[act] [curio] interact with travellers_tent_tutorial bare-handed   KEY W
[act] [curio] no panel on screen after W
   ...  x3, each followed by "one more step and trying again"
[act] [curio] a fight started on the way
```

**Nothing in the bridge is broken here, and three plausible culprits are
ruled out by the evidence:**

- **`W` is the right key.** It is `dungeon_interact_with_curio` from the
  binary's input table, and the SAME key opened a panel for `sconce` in the
  same run (`panel is up - clicking the hand`). The panel detector works.
- **The battle check works.** `_nudge` saw the fight and said so; the loop
  banked its frame and handed over to the route, exactly as designed.
- **The save agrees the party is on the curio's tile** -- `at tile 4,
  travellers_tent_tutorial (tile 4)`.

**What the frames show is a DISTANCE problem.** `captures/_curio_approach.png`
has the party standing clearly to the LEFT of the tent with a gap between
them; `Area.walked` is tile granularity and the tent's tile is wide, so "on
the tile" is not "in reach". The three nudges then walk 0.9 s each and the
encounter beyond the tent triggers before the gap closes --
`captures/_curio_miss.png` is a combat, with brigands where the tent was.

**Fran's rule, given on seeing the frame:** *"the curio needs to be a bit to
the right of the party, you cant interact with curio on top of them."* That is
the game's own semantics -- `tutorial_popup_using_doors_description` says
"press [W] when standing in front of it" -- and it is already what
`_walk_up_to`'s comment reasons about.

**And it does NOT explain this failure, which is what makes the frame
valuable.** In `captures/_curio_approach.png` the tent IS a bit to the right:
the front hero ends around x 650 and the tent starts around x 690 at 1280
wide. The position satisfies the rule and `W` still opened nothing.

**A FOURTH position theory would therefore be the wrong move.** Three have
already been falsified here -- unconditional press, stop one tile short (the
`ahead <= 1` version, rejected because a banked frame showed the party short
anyway), and walk onto the tile (current). Tonight's frame falsifies "get
closer" as a complete explanation too.

**What the evidence now points at is the PANEL, not the position.** The same
run pressed `W` on a `sconce` and logged `panel is up - clicking the hand at
(798, 691)`; the tent logged `no panel on screen after W - either it did not
open, or it is not where the detector looks`. Same key, same actuator, same
raid, one curio works and one does not -- so the difference is the curio, and
the bridge's own message already names the live branch. A travellers' tent is
a camping curio rather than a loot curio, and there is no reason its panel has
to be drawn where a sconce's is.

Next step is one frame, not a code change: press `W` at the tent and capture
what is actually on screen at that moment, rather than asking the sconce
detector whether it recognises it.

## The torch layer blocked the route, and it cost a raid (2026-09-01)

`plan_room`'s own docstring states the rule: **"A layer that cannot act never
blocks one that can."** It was written when the curio branch stranded the
party. The TORCH branch kept returning, and it did the same thing.

Live, in `rooH` of a generated Ruins quest:

```
TORCH   torch 72 (dim) - RELIGHT: dark outside the killing blow is a straight loss
  [act] [torch] relight before moving on      KEY  T   use a torch
  [act] no save change in 15s - the input did not land (attempt 1/3)
  [act] frame banked to captures/_send_miss.png
  ...  (attempt 3/3)
  [act] giving up on this turn - play it yourself.
[night] play ended: stalled 180s
```

The actuator gave up on the turn exactly as designed -- and then the party
stood still until the watchdog killed the iteration, because **`plan_room`
checks the torch FIRST**, so every following poll re-planned the same relight
and the route step was never reached. 40 actions into a raid, thrown away.

**The banked frame shows nothing covering the screen** -- no modal, no popup,
seven torches in the pack. And the game ships
`str_cant_use_torch_at_limit`: *"It's no use...this is all the light we are
getting for now."* Fran, on seeing it: *"There was already full torch, using a
torch on over 75 torch value its pointless."* `RADIANT_FLOOR` is 76.0 and the
relight gate is `torch < 76`, so the DECISION matches his rule; what the save
reported (72) and what the screen showed (full) did not agree, and that
disagreement is still open.

**Which is precisely why an unacknowledged torch has to be survivable rather
than fatal.** The fix is not to guess at the torch reading: it is that a layer
which cannot act must hand over to one that can. `_CurioDone` is generalised
to `_LayerDone(update, *fields)` and `_torch_done` strikes the torch out the
same way `_curio_done` strikes the curio out, so the route gets its turn.
`_AdviceWithout.empty` is recomputed over what REMAINS -- reading it off the
underlying advice would say "still busy" forever, which is the same stall in a
different costume.

Three tests pin it, including that the two proxies blank only their own field:
they share a `_BLANK` table now, which is exactly where that would go wrong.
